74.11 Library drift noticed while closing this section

  fg_library.yaml has been edited outside the agent's changes
  since section 73 - presumably through the GUI, since the
  explanatory comments were dropped as well:

  * phytoplankton.seed_rate is 0.0 AGAIN. Section 73 set it to
    1e-07 precisely to remove the absorbing zero at the base of
    the food web; that is now undone, and
    tests/test_absorbing_zero.py::
    test_phytoplankton_seed_rate_is_enabled_in_the_library is
    RED as a result (147 passed, 1 failed). The float32 harvest
    cap (MAX_HARVEST_FRAC) is still in place, so a cell is no
    longer zeroed in a single tick - but if a cell does reach
    zero there is again no way back, and 69.6's warning about
    retraining in a dying world applies again. NOT reverted:
    it may be a deliberate experiment. Decide explicitly.
  * Two new pair overrides appeared. CORRECTION: an earlier
    draft of this entry attributed the 0.25 to seabirds. That
    was a misreading of the diff - no seabirds_preys_on_* entry
    carries a visibility_floor at all, and seabirds, seals and
    porpoises all sit on the species default 0.0. The complete
    set of pair overrides in the library is exactly three:

      gadoids_preys_on_pelagic_fish     0.25
      porpoises_preys_on_pelagic_fish   0.95
      porpoises_preys_on_gadoids        0.75

  * gadoids_preys_on_pelagic_fish 0.25 is BELOW the herring
    species default 0.35, i.e. it makes cod worse at seeing
    hidden herring than the generic case, which TIGHTENS the
    cod -> herring budget rather than relaxing it. 74.2 lists
    that pair at sat_min = 0.117 -> 0.13 t visible per cell; at
    floor 0.25 instead of 0.35 the same visible density needs
    more total herring when part of the school is rested, so the
    total requirement rises by the ratio of the floors on the
    hidden fraction. The pair is far from its break-even, so
    this is not load-bearing for the section 74 argument - but
    it is a real, deliberate-looking calibration and should be
    motivated in the YAML comment like the porpoise one is.
  * porpoises_preys_on_gadoids 0.75 touches the pair 74.2b
    calls arithmetically infeasible - note that visibility_floor
    cannot rescue it: sat_min = 1.227 is independent of prey
    density, so raising visibility does not change the verdict.
    Only a, energy_gain or the hunger gate can.
  * porpoises_preys_on_pelagic_fish is at 0.95, not the 1.0 the
    YAML comment used to claim, and the old "15.1 to 5.3" figure
    was stale as well. FIXED: the comment now states floor 0.95,
    a break-even of 5.25 t visible / ~5.6 t total per cell
    against 15.1 t/cell at the herring species default 0.35, and
    cites sections 71.12 and 74.2. It also no longer says
    "detectable to seabirds" - the 0.35 is the herring species
    default that applies to every predator without an override,
    seabirds have nothing to do with it.

75 THE MERGE WITH origin/coev, AND THE VISUALISER IT BROKE
    The refactor branch (origin/coev, 8 commits, "break out env to
    multiple files" + PR #12) replaced the 1577-line monolithic
    lib/environments/ecosystem.py with a shim over the package
    lib/environments/ecosystem_env/*. The merge kept the modular
    architecture and ported the missing behaviour into it (impacts,
    MAX_HARVEST_FRAC, pair visibility floors, split suppression,
    accessibility mask, full extinction threshold, no-arg step(),
    pi_* publication); see the merge commit body for the list. The
    remote had also rewritten lib/viz/pygame_viz.py, and that part
    was a regression the user reported immediately:
    "visualiseringen har forstorts".

75.1 What the remote viz changed
    Two unrelated things, both user-visible:
    (a) Fixed colour/axis reference. Heatmaps stopped auto-scaling
        to the current frame max and were normalised against the
        ROLLOUT-START per-cell max times a new global
        "Biomass scale" slider, which defaulted to 5.0x. The
        biomass plot y-axis was likewise locked to 0..100*scale,
        i.e. 0..500 %. Consequence on a run that drifts even a
        little below its start density: every heatmap renders as
        uniform dark purple and every curve is squashed onto the
        bottom 20 % of the plot. That is exactly the screenshot the
        user attached.
    (b) The "impacts" plot tab, the "im" field in the pr/st/im line
        above each heatmap and the impact series in the saved HTML
        were deleted, even though loss_impact is still accumulated
        by the environment (and was restored by this merge).

75.2 Resolution (user-chosen)
    (a) Auto-scaling is the DEFAULT again. The slider survives but
        is an opt-in override: _biomass_scale_manual starts False
        and _biomass_display_scale starts at 1.0; the label reads
        "Biomass scale = auto" and the knob is greyed out until the
        user drags it. Touching it goes through
        _set_biomass_display_scale() which pins both the heatmap
        reference (start max * scale) and the biomass y-axis
        (0..100*scale). Clicking the label calls
        _reset_biomass_display_scale() and returns to auto - that is
        the only way back, so the click target is registered in
        _biomass_scale_label_rect and tested before the slider track
        in _handle_click.
        The fixed reference is still worth having: it keeps colours
        comparable across ticks and keeps a near-extinct FG dark
        instead of re-expanding its residual biomass to full yellow.
        It is just wrong as a default.
    (b) impacts restored end to end: tab + label in both modes,
        'impact' key in update_loss_breakdown, the pr/st/im header
        line, the NDM filter in _active_plot_ids (NDMs lose biomass
        to impacts too, so they belong on the loss tabs), and the
        impacts series in _save_inference_plot_html. Producers were
        re-wired as well: the per-tick push in inference.py and the
        end-of-probe push in train.py (including the _rnd baseline
        and the --resume hydration from biomass.jsonl).

    seeded passive currents", merged through a41548e) was never in
    coev, which is why "are currents active during inference?" had to
    be answered with "they do not exist" - correct for the checkout,
    stale for the repository. Merged with --no-ff and NO conflicts.
    His commit DELETES mareld_resume.txt (6476 lines); the file was
    restored from e27c289 before the merge commit, so the merge keeps
    it. The activation fix (inference.py reading
    architecture.activation, train.py writing it - see the arc right
    before this section) was stashed across the merge and committed
    on top of it as 2d3c63a. New in the tree:

    lib/runners/population_stability.py, lib/runners/profiles.py,
    CURRENTS.md, POPULATION_STABILITY.md, plus --currents on /

    and benchmark_gpu.py.

76.1 Three tests were RED on his branch too - all three now fixed
    Verified by checking out a41548e in a separate worktree: the
    failures are his, not the merge's. Two independent causes.

    (a) currents.py was not torch.compile-traceable (torch 2.1).
        test_currents.py::test_current_training_is_chunk_independent
        _and_traces_without_readback died inside
        torch.compile(fullgraph=True) on
            world_seed ^ config.seed
        Dynamo lowers "tensor ^ python_int" to aten::bitwise_xor.
        Tensor, which refuses the scalar ("Unable to cast 0 to
        Tensor"). Measured in isolation: &, >>, * and tensor ^ tensor
        all trace fine, only xor-with-scalar breaks, and builtin abs()
        on a tensor is unsupported as well. FIX: local helpers _xor()
        (promotes either side with torch.as_tensor when the other side
        is a tensor) and _abs() (torch.abs for tensors), used by
        _hash32() and by the N/E/S/W fractions. Numerics unchanged -
        the CPU/GPU parity and reproducibility tests in
        test_currents.py still hold (17/17 green).

    (b) RolloutRunner.observed_ticks was mis-scheduled under Inductor.
        test_gpu_trainer.py::test_cuda_execution_matches_eager_and
        _replay_advances[stability1-compile] and [...-compile-graph]
        reported rewards off by 1.76e-2 against rtol 1e-4. Root cause
        was NOT tolerance: _tick() did
            alive = ~self.failed
            self.observed_ticks.add_(alive)
        and later, in the same traced graph,
        self.failed.logical_or_(breach). A one-tick experiment on
        cuda: eager gives observed=[1,1,1,1,...], execution=compile
        gives [0,1,1,0,...] - exactly the worlds that breached on that
        tick lose their increment, i.e. the compiled kernel reads the
        ALREADY UPDATED flag. cuda-graph was bit-identical; only the
        Inductor paths drifted. Moving the logical_or_ after the last
        use of alive did NOT help (the dependency is on the buffer,
        not the source order), and a minimal 4-element repro does not
        reproduce it, so this is a torch 2.1 Inductor scheduling
        quirk, not a source-order bug. obs_sum masking with the same
        alive was NOT affected.
        FIX: stop accumulating the counter. observed_ticks is now a
        read-only property
            valid_ticks + failed.long()
        (horizon when population stability is off), which is exact:
        a world is alive at the start of every tick until it
        breaches, so it observes its valid ticks plus the single
        failing tick. The buffer, its reset and its increment are
        gone, so nothing reads "failed" inside the traced tick any
        more.
        WHY IT MOVED THE REWARDS: observed_ticks is the sample count
        for ARS-V2 observation normalisation (lib/gpu/trainer.py,
        observation_count). A count that is one too low per failing
        world skews obs_mean/obs_var, which moves the policies, which
        is then amplified by the test's degenerate warning band
        (lower=0.99, warning_lower=1.0 gives a gain of
        1/(1.0-0.99) = 100 on relative biomass deviations). The
        0.0176 was a consequence of the bad count, not float noise -
        with the fix the same test is green over repeated runs.

76.2 Verification
    pytest tests/ -q -> 342 passed, 12 skipped, 4 failed. The 4 are
    the PRE-EXISTING failures in
    test_gpu_ecosystem.py::test_full_ticks_match_reference (all four
    parametrisations): energy_reserve, 1 of 80 elements, max abs
    1.0e-3 / max rel 2.6e-4 against atol=rtol=4e-5.
    CORRECTION (see section 77): the "float32 cancellation in the
    accumulated gain-minus-cost term" diagnosis originally written
    here was WRONG. The failures are a real formula difference - the
    GPU predation kept the pre-Section-73 harvest cap - and they are
    gone after the fixes in 77.
    Also: 1-generation GPU training with
    --currents on --population-stability --execution compile
    completes, and a 1-generation CPU run with --currents on followed
    by inference.py --currents on loads every policy as 2x16 tanh,
    i.e. the activation fix and the current code coexist.

76.3 Open
    The two fixes in 76.1 are uncommitted (currents.py, rollout.py),
    as is this section. Nothing here addresses the porpoise collapse
    - but --population-stability is precisely a scoring mode for it,
    and --currents on changes the foraging problem by moving the
    plankton, so both are now available for that work.

77 THE GPU ENGINE WAS OPTIMISING A DIFFERENT BIOLOGY
    The 4 remaining test_gpu_ecosystem.py failures were NOT float32
    parity (76.2 was wrong). lib/gpu/ecosystem.py had drifted three
    ecological mechanisms behind lib/environments/ecosystem_env/, so
    train_gpu.py maximised a reward computed under different rules
    than inference.py / the CPU trainer then run - the same class of
    bug as the activation mismatch, but in the dynamics instead of
    the network. All three are fixed; the whole suite is green
    (354 passed, 12 skipped, 0 failed).

77.1 How the test pointed straight at it
    Only handling=0.0 failed; both handling=0.2 parametrisations were
    green with 50x margin, and the divergence was already there in
    TICK 0 (R absmax 1.043e-03 at handling=0.0 vs 1.907e-06 at
    handling=0.2). Three orders of magnitude at the first step is a
    formula difference, not accumulation. Reason for the split: with
    handling_time = 0 there is no Holling saturation (a_eff = a), so
    demand is unbounded, exceeds the prey biomass and activates the
    over-harvest branch; with handling=0.2 intake saturates,
    total <= visible always, both engines take scale = 1.0 and the
    differing code is never executed. The test hits exactly the edge
    case where the two formulas differ.

77.2 Fix 1 - the harvest cap (the actual test failure)
    CPU (predation.py):
        harvestable = visible * MAX_HARVEST_FRAC      # 0.999
        scale = where(total > harvestable,
                      harvestable / max(total, 1e-30), 1.0)
    GPU (ecosystem.py:187, before):
        scale = where(total > visible, visible / (total + 1e-9), 1.0)
    i.e. the pre-Section-73 form: cap at 100% of the visible prey
    instead of 99.9%, and an absolute epsilon in the denominator
    instead of a guarded division. The ratio of the two scales is
    exactly 0.999, i.e. 1e-3 relative in the intake - the observed
    magnitude. MAX_HARVEST_FRAC is now IMPORTED into
    lib/gpu/ecosystem.py (HARVEST_FRACTION) so the two engines cannot
    drift apart again. This alone removed 2 of the 4 failures; the
    other 2 dropped 10x and then disappeared as well once 77.3
    landed.

77.3 Fix 2 - per-pair visibility_floor and per-FG satiation_scale
    Two silent divergences the old fixture never exercised (it has no
    pair override and no satiation override, so both engines took the
    same cheap path):
    (a) Per-(predator, prey) visibility_floor did not exist on the
        GPU at all: it used only _all_visibility_floor (the prey's
        own floor), while the CPU has vis_floor_mat, the per-predator
        demand bound min(demand, pair_visible) and a shared
        availability cap = max over the predator rows that actually
        eat that prey. fg_library.yaml carries three overrides
        (porpoises->pelagic_fish 0.95, porpoises->gadoids 0.75,
        gadoids->pelagic_fish 0.25), so under GPU training the
        porpoises saw hiding herring with floor 0.35 instead of their
        biosonar 0.95 - about a third of the hidden prey - which
        points straight into the porpoise collapse (section 71.12:
        break-even 15.1 t/cell at 0.35 against 5.3 t/cell at full
        detectability).
    (b) satiation_scale was hardcoded as /0.8 on the GPU; the CPU
        resolves it per FG (resolve_satiation_scale), and
        fg_library.yaml sets 0.9 for porpoises.
    Implementation: TensorEcosystem gains pair_visibility
    (None = keep the bit-identical shared-vector path, exactly the
    _has_pair_vis_floor switch the reference makes) and satiation
    (through the shared resolver). predation() mirrors the CPU order:
    pair_visible -> shared cap by amax over eat_mask rows -> Holling
    on the pair-visible prey -> min(demand, pair_visible) ->
    MAX_HARVEST_FRAC scaling.
    OBSERVATION SIDE TOO: the CPU resolves the floor per OBSERVER
    (_obs_vis_floor), so the GPU observation bank now carries D
    blocks of G visible channels instead of one, indexed from
    _build_indices. The per-DM input dimension (max_in_dim) is
    unchanged, so checkpoints stay valid; the extra cost is D*G*C
    floats per world in the bank (60x60, G=8, D=6, 32 worlds ->
    ~22 MB) and only for projects that actually declare an override.

77.4 Tests
    test_gpu_ecosystem.py: make_env() takes pair_floors= and
    satiation=, the 12-tick parity loop is extracted as
    _assert_parity(env, device, ticks), and a new
    test_pair_visibility_and_satiation_match_reference exercises the
    new paths with floor in {0.0, 0.95} x handling in {0.0, 0.2}.
    floor=0.0 is deliberate: it is the case where a non-preying row
    would inflate the shared cap if the amax forgot to mask by
    eat_mask. test_full_ticks_match_reference now also asserts
    pair_visibility is None, i.e. projects without an override keep
    the cheap path.
    The new test runs 6 ticks, not 12. Here the engines really do
    differ by one float32 ulp in tick 0 (the operand grouping is not
    identical) and the feedback through the policy input amplifies it
    chaotically in the single cell where prey is harvested down to
    the 0.1% residue: measured on CUDA rel 1e-7 (tick 0) -> 6e-5
    (tick 6) -> 8e-5 (tick 11). Six ticks stay two orders below the
    tolerance and still catch a formula difference, which announces
    itself at tick 0 and 1000x larger.

77.5 Verification and consequences
    pytest tests/ -q -> 354 passed, 12 skipped. 1-generation GPU
    training on mareld2.yaml (16x16, 2x16 tanh) completes with both
    --execution compile and --execution cuda-graph and gives
    identical rewards, so the new branches trace and capture. On the
    real project the pair path is now active: the GPU sees
    porpoises->pelagic_fish 0.95 and porpoises satiation 0.9.
    CONSEQUENCE: every existing GPU-trained policy was trained
    against the wrong predation - too little hidden prey detectable
    for the porpoises, a slightly too permissive harvest cap and the
    wrong satiation gate. The checkpoint FORMAT is unchanged (no
    observation-dimension change), but the numbers behind those
    weights are. Rerun anything whose conclusion depends on porpoise
    predation.

78 CURRENTS COULD NOT BE CAPTURED INTO A CUDA GRAPH
    Symptom: train_gpu.py --currents on with the DEFAULT
    --execution cuda-graph aborted in RolloutRunner.prepare() with
        torch.AcceleratorError: CUDA error: operation not permitted
        when stream is capturing   (cudaErrorStreamCaptureUnsupported)
    followed by cudaErrorStreamCaptureInvalidated from
    capture_end(). Traceback ended in currents._xor ->
    torch.as_tensor(...). Progress probe and the first survival.jsonl
    write had already succeeded, so it was purely the capture.

78.1 Root cause
    The Dynamo fix from section 76 promoted the Python hash
    constants with
        torch.as_tensor(scalar, dtype=..., device=cuda)
    which builds the scalar on the HOST and then issues a
    cudaMemcpyAsync from pageable host memory. Allocation from the
    caching allocator is legal during capture (private pool), a H2D
    copy from pageable memory is NOT. Hence the split behaviour:
    eager and --execution compile worked (only the graph CAPTURE is
    restricted), cuda-graph and compile-graph did not. _hash32 runs
    four xors per current vector and direction_fractions calls it
    for two epochs, so it tripped on the very first tick captured.

78.2 Fix
    New helper currents._promote(value, like): tensors are only
    dtype-cast, Python/NumPy scalars become
    torch.full((), int(value), dtype, device). torch.full lowers to
    empty + fill_ with the value as a KERNEL ARGUMENT, so no host
    memory is touched and capture is safe. The numerics are
    unchanged (same int64 arithmetic), so existing runs are
    comparable. lib/gpu/ecosystem.py::advect got the same treatment
    for a scalar ``tick`` argument, which only the non-rollout
    callers pass.

78.3 Test
    tests/test_currents.py::
    test_current_training_captures_into_a_cuda_graph
    (cuda-graph and compile-graph, skipped without a GPU) captures a
    graph with currents enabled and compares rewards against eager
    over ticks 7 and 4 (remainder plus a changed horizon). Verified
    to FAIL with the old as_tensor form and pass with the fix. The
    existing current tests only used eager/compile, which is exactly
    why the hole existed: --execution is what decides whether the
    same code path is captured or merely traced.

78.4 Verification
    pytest tests/ -q -> 356 passed, 12 skipped. The reported command
    (16x16, 2x48 tanh, --currents on --migration on --mortality off
    --profile info, cuda-graph) now runs; 200-tick, 16-delta,
    3-world iterations complete in ~0.8 s/update after warmup.
    Note: porpoises score about -12 there, i.e. the population
    collapse of section 77.5 is still open and unrelated to this
    fix.

79 LOCAL PER-CELL SOURCE-TRACKED REWARD (--local_reward)
    The default fitness is one global scalar,
    mean_t log(E_total(t)/E_total(0)) with
    E_total = sum_c (B(c)*energy_content + R(c)). It is dominated by
    the big cells: managing a 1000-ton cell well and a 1-kg cell
    badly scores the same as the reverse, so most of the grid
    carries almost no gradient.

    The local reward (idea proposed in localreward.txt, sharpened in
    review) scores every cell separately, per tick:

        A(c,t)   = B(c,t)*energy_content + R(c,t)   at tick START
        B(c,t+1) = sum_d share_d(c) * Q(dest(c,d), t+1)
        reward(c)= B(c,t+1) / A(c,t)

    d runs over the plus-shaped destination set {c, N, E, S, W} --
    five terms, not the nine of a 3x3 square: MOVE_SLICE is
    slice(0,4) and _transfer_to_neighbours moves exactly one step in
    N/E/S/W, so diagonals are unreachable in one tick. The quotient
    is scale invariant, hence a small population's decision counts
    as much as a large one's. That was the whole point of the
    proposal.

79.1 Why the proportional allocation is EXACT, not an approximation
    Order in environment.step():
        impact_mortality -> predation -> apply_energy_costs
        -> apply_movement -> apply_currents -> population_change
        -> accessibility_mask -> extinction_threshold
    * Impacts and predation run BEFORE the movement, so those losses
      are already booked in the source cell. Nothing to track, and
      this is what makes "my population got eaten" register as a low
      reward(c) for the cell that made the decision.
    * apply_movement yields five contributions per source cell, all
      indexed on the source: b_stay (rest + eat + the non-moving
      share) and b_out[:, d] for d in {N,E,S,W}.
    * apply_currents touches only non decision makers
      (ids = [f for f in ... if not is_decision_maker]), so it never
      perturbs the DM tracking.
    * population_change, the accessibility mask, the extinction
      sweep and the reserve clip np.clip(r_total, 0, max_reserve)
      are all CELL-WISE MULTIPLICATIVE. Therefore
      unit(c) = Q_end(c) / b_total(c) is exactly what one delivered
      ton is worth at the end of the tick, and splitting a
      destination's end energy between its sources in proportion to
      the biomass each delivered is an identity.
    Implementation: five slice-assign gathers per DM per tick, the
    same pattern as _transfer_to_neighbours, negligible next to the
    policy forward pass.

79.2 Edge cases that had to be decided
    * suppress_subthreshold_splits (section 69) cancels outflows
      that would land in a still-sub-threshold cell and returns them
      to the source. The contributions MUST be read after that
      correction, otherwise the mass balance does not close. The
      function therefore grew an optional return_cancelled=True that
      also hands back b_cancel/r_cancel; apply_movement folds them
      back into b_stay and out of b_out. Default call signature
      unchanged (2-tuple), so the legacy alias
      _suppress_subthreshold_splits and its tests are untouched.
    * Off-grid outflow has no in-grid destination. Counting it as a
      loss would punish border cells for a move the tracker cannot
      follow, so instead A is scaled by the in-grid biomass fraction
      frac_in(c) = (b_stay + sum_{d in grid} b_out) / (b_stay + sum_d
      b_out). Note the default action mask already removes off-grid
      directions, so this only bites with --migration on.
    * With --migration on the immigrating biomass has no source.
      Because the shares are normalised by the destination's TOTAL
      post-movement biomass, the immigrated part is attributed to
      nobody; consequently sum_c B(c,t+1) < sum_c Q(c,t+1) there.
      The exact mass-balance identity holds with --migration off.

79.3 The aggregation is the real design decision
    A cell-weighted sum telescopes: with w_c = A_c / sum A_c the
    local reward IS the global one (this was the original objection,
    and it is correct). With w_c = 1 it does not telescope, and
    sum_c reward(c) is genuinely new information. But the raw sum has
    N_occ(t) terms, each around 1.0, so
        Reward ~ N_occ * (average local growth rate)
    and the net can raise it simply by occupying more cells -- a
    nearly empty cell just above the viability threshold contributes
    as much as a 1000-ton one. The only brake in the code is
    suppress_subthreshold_splits plus apply_extinction_threshold, so
    the optimum becomes "spread as thin as possible but just above
    thr". That may be ecologically sound (ideal free distribution)
    or degenerate; it is the first thing to A/B test.
    Hence both are flags rather than a hardcoded choice:
        --local_reward_norm  mean|sum   (default mean)
        --local_reward_theta THETA      w_c = A_c**theta, default 0
    theta=0 + sum  = the literal proposal.
    theta=0 + mean = average local growth rate, no spread gradient.
    theta=1 + mean = collapses back onto the global energy growth
                     rate (asserted by a test), so ONE flag value
                     gives both baselines under identical CRN seeds.
    Metric: --local_reward_metric log|ratio. The raw quotient is
    asymmetric (doubling +1.0, halving -0.5), i.e. mildly risk
    LOVING, which premieres boom-bust. log(B/A) is symmetric and
    optimises geometric-mean growth (Kelly), which is risk averse
    and matches POPULATION_STABILITY.md. Default log.
    Clipping: --local_reward_clip LO HI on the ratio (default
    0.2 2.0) and --local_reward_min_energy_factor, which restricts
    the cell set to A_c >= factor * extinction_threshold_factor *
    min_split_biomass * energy_content, i.e. cells that held a
    viable population at tick start. Defining the cell set at t (not
    t+1) is what keeps float32 residue out of the quotient.

79.4 On gamma=0 and one-tick credit
    A one-tick reward cannot see that a move pays off at t+2: eat
    happens before the movement and the move cost is charged in
    apply_energy_costs, so a cell that puts its mass on move gets no
    intake and a full cost that tick. In ARS this matters less than
    it looks: fitness is a sum over ALL ticks, so the destination
    cell contributes its own high quotient at t+1 and the credit is
    merely booked anonymously in the next term. ARS needs no
    per-step credit assignment (no value function, no gamma), so a
    K-step backward pass over saved share matrices would only reduce
    variance, not enable movement to pay. It was therefore left out.
    Note that --local_reward changes the SHAPE of the fitness
    landscape (from "maximise total energy" to "maximise the typical
    cell's growth rate, spatially risk averse"); motivate it
    ecologically, not by credit assignment, per AGENTS.md section 7.

79.5 Where it is wired in
    * lib/environments/ecosystem_env/source_tracking.py (new):
      LocalRewardConfig, local_reward_options(args), attach/reset,
      begin_tick / record_movement / end_tick, fitness, occupancy.
    * movement.apply_movement publishes the corrected per-source
      flow; suppress_subthreshold_splits gained return_cancelled.
    * environment.step() snapshots A before apply_impact_mortality
      and cashes in the shares after apply_extinction_threshold.
      The env ctor takes local_reward=None, so the tick is unchanged
      when the flag is off (asserted by a test).
    * lib/runners/trainer.py: self.local_reward plus
      _local_reward_payload() in ALL FOUR dict-task blocks
      (train_step M=1 and M>1, train_step_coevolution M=1 and M>1)
      and both sequential paths (_evaluate, _evaluate_coevo).
      IMPORTANT: --local_reward, like the energy reward, must force
      the dict-task; the byte-identical tuple-task path now requires
      legacy_reward and local_reward is None.
    * lib/runners/parallel_worker.py: task.get('local_reward'),
      source_tracking.attach on the fresh env, and a fitness branch
      in both _evaluate_task and _evaluate_coevo_task.
    * lib/runners/population_stability.py: validate_reward grew a
      local_reward argument; mutually exclusive with
      --legacy_reward and --population-stability.
    * train.py: the flags, local_reward_options, the banner, the
      __meta__ header in biomass.jsonl, and the random-action
      baseline (which mirrors the reward formula by hand -- forget
      it and the *_rnd curve is plotted against the wrong formula).
    * tools/biomass_html.py: _reward_title_from_meta labels the
      reward tab.
    * GPU: mirrored in section 80. When 79 was written lib/gpu/*
      was NOT mirrored and --local_reward was a train.py flag only.

79.6 Diagnostics
    env.local_reward_sum / local_reward_occupied / local_reward_ticks
    accumulate per FG alongside loss_predation / loss_starvation, and
    env.local_reward_last carries this tick's per-cell start,
    tracked, frac_in, active, ratio and value arrays. occupancy() is
    the mean number of participating cells per tick -- log it, since
    a runaway N_occ is exactly the spread gradient of 79.3.

79.7 Verification
    tests/test_local_reward.py (15 tests): mass balance on one tick
    and over five, pure-rest degeneracy, ratio 1.0 for a full 4-way
    split, border/off-grid handling, a swept cell scoring finite
    rather than NaN, viability gating, bit-identical dynamics with
    the flag off, theta=1 collapsing onto the global growth rate,
    sum-vs-mean cell-count scaling, config validation and the CLI
    wiring. pytest tests/ -q -> 371 passed, 12 skipped.
    Real-world check on mareld2.yaml (60x60, 15 ticks, random
    policies): the mass balance holds to < 2e-4 relative for every
    DM every tick with predation, impacts and real thresholds
    active. Raw values, log/mean: gadoids -0.0008 (occ 1743),
    pelagic_fish -0.0006 (occ 2477), porpoises -0.1207 (occ 251),
    zooplankton +0.0019 (occ 3600); the same rollout's global
    cumulative reward is -0.0058 / -0.0031 / -0.5064 / +0.0164, so
    the local signal ranks the species the same way while being a
    per-tick quantity. With ratio/sum the values become ~N_occ as
    predicted in 79.3, which is the spread gradient made visible.
    1-generation training runs complete in both co-evolution and
    round-robin mode.

79.8 Recommended next steps
    1. Log occupancy() next to the reward in the probe rollout and
       watch whether N_occ runs away under --local_reward_norm sum.
    2. A/B --local_reward_theta 0 against 1 with the same CRN seed;
       theta=1 is the global baseline by construction.
    3. Only then consider a K-step backward pass over the saved
       share matrices (--local_reward_horizon), which is a variance
       reduction, not a correctness fix (see 79.4).


80 --local_reward ON THE GPU PATH (mirroring section 79)
    train_gpu.py now accepts the same --local_reward family as
    train.py and optimises the same fitness. The two engines agree to
    six decimals on identical rollouts (80.4), which is the whole
    point: before this, --gpu runs silently optimised the global
    reward while the CPU runs optimised the local one.

80.1 What the tensor engine made easy
    The GPU tick is
        predation -> movement -> advect -> population
    i.e. no impact mortality and no suppress_subthreshold_splits, so
    the tracking is the plain five-term variant of 79.1 with no
    cancellation correction to read back. Everything the tracker
    needs was already computed inside movement():
        b_stay  = b*rest + b*eat + (moving_b - b_out).sum(2)
        b_out   [E, D, 4, C]  outflow per source cell and direction
        b_total [E, D, C]     post-movement biomass per cell
    Previously b_stay was not named; b_total was simply initialised
    with that expression and then accumulated into. Naming it costs
    nothing and makes the flow available.

80.2 Direction indexing (the one place to get wrong)
    The settle loop reads, for direction d, index = neighbors[(d+2)%4],
    i.e. cell c receives the direction-d outflow of the cell
    neighbors[opp(d)][c]. Inverting that, the destination of a
    direction-d move FROM the source is neighbors[d][source]. The
    tracker therefore gathers the destination's per-unit energy with
    unit[:, :, neighbors[direction]] * neighbor_valid[direction] --
    the same index tensors, read in the opposite direction. Getting
    this backwards is silent: mass balance still holds, but every
    moving cell is credited with the WRONG neighbour.

80.3 Where it is wired in
    * lib/gpu/ecosystem.py: movement(..., track=False) returns a
      third value (b_stay, b_out, b_total) when asked; local_energy()
      is Q(c) per DM; tracked_energy() does the proportional
      allocation plus frac_in; local_min_energy(factor) is the
      viability floor -- threshold already stores
      extinction_threshold_factor * min_split_biomass, the same
      product the reference reads from _dm_split_thr.
      step(..., track_source=True) returns a sixth value
      (start, tracked, frac_in); start is taken BEFORE predation,
      i.e. the state the policy observed, exactly as begin_tick does.
    * lib/gpu/rollout.py: LocalRewardConfig on the runner, the
      float64 buffers local_sum / local_occupied, the aggregation in
      _accumulate_local_reward (clip -> metric -> theta weight ->
      mean|sum, in float64), results() returning local_sum/ticks, and
      the occupancy property. validate_reward is called with the
      config, so --legacy-reward and --population-stability are
      rejected here too.
    * lib/gpu/trainer.py: local_reward in runner_options, plus a
      local_occupancy metric written to training.jsonl -- the GPU
      answer to 79.8 step 1.
    * lib/gpu/cli.py + train_gpu.py: the flags and the banner.
    * The flags themselves moved into
      source_tracking.add_local_reward_arguments(parser), used by
      BOTH train.py and lib/gpu/cli.py, so the two entry points
      cannot drift apart on names, choices or defaults (same pattern
      as add_current_arguments / add_population_arguments).

80.4 Verification
    tests/test_gpu_local_reward.py (27 cases incl. CUDA params):
    mass balance sum_c B = sum_c Q(t+1) with and without natural
    mortality, pure rest degenerating to the cell's own energy,
    rollout fitness vs ARSTrainer._evaluate_coevo for four configs
    (log/mean, ratio/sum, ratio/mean/theta=1, tightened clip plus
    min_energy_factor) x migration on/off, the tracked path leaving
    the dynamics bit-identical, and torch.compile(fullgraph=True)
    tracing the tracked tick without a graph break (required for
    cuda-graph execution). Measured parity on a 4x5 fixture, 6 ticks:
        log/mean    GPU -0.053543 / -0.079778  CPU identical
        ratio/sum   GPU 18.80517 / 17.587451   CPU identical
        ratio/th=1  GPU 0.946493 / 0.925788    CPU identical
    with occupancy 19.8 / 19.0 of 20 cells, so the comparison is not
    vacuous. pytest tests/ -q -> 398 passed, 12 skipped.
    train_gpu.py --local_reward, 1 generation, 12x12, device cpu:
    reward_mean gadoids -0.0802, pelagic_fish -0.1120, porpoises
    -0.2046, zooplankton -0.1332 with local_occupancy 141.7 / 144.0 /
    4.4 / 143.8 of 144 cells -- porpoises occupy few cells, which is
    precisely the asymmetry the local reward is meant to expose.

80.5 Kept deliberately out
    * Test fixtures: make_env gained min_split / extinction_factor
      arguments (defaults unchanged). The old defaults sit ABOVE the
      fixture's biomass, so splits are suppressed and the extinction
      sweep clears the grid; a tracking test would compare zeros.
      Zero thresholds also keep the reference's
      suppress_subthreshold_splits inactive, which the tensor engine
      did not implement -- a genuine CPU/GPU discrepancy that is
      older than this arc (see section 69) and was not touched here.
      FIXED afterwards in section 81, which mirrors the suppression on
      the GPU path and adds a fixture where it actually fires.
    * --migration on still excludes immigrated biomass (no source
      cell) on both paths, so the mass-balance identity is exact only
      with migration off. The parity test covers migration on anyway:
      both engines exclude it the same way.
    * No K-step backward pass (79.4) on either path.

81. APPLIED: SUB-THRESHOLD SPLIT SUPPRESSION ON THE GPU PATH
    (CPU/GPU parity, closes the gap left open in 80.5)

81.1 The discrepancy
    Section 69 added movement.suppress_subthreshold_splits to the
    reference tick: population_change.apply_extinction_threshold
    zeroes every cell that ends the tick below
        thr = extinction_threshold_factor * min_split_biomass
    so a decision maker that splits its moving share across up to
    four directions bleeds biomass through that sweep (measured for
    porpoises in 68.4: 25.1 of 40 ton to the sweep vs. 14.9 ton to
    starvation). The fix cancels an outflow whose DESTINATION would
    still be below thr after receiving it; the biomass and its
    reserve stay in the source cell.

    lib/gpu/ecosystem.py never had that step. TensorEcosystem.movement
    went straight from the settle loop (plus migration) to the reserve
    clip, so train_gpu.py optimised against a diffusion loss that
    train.py and inference.py do not have. Both engines apply the
    sweep itself (population(): b < threshold -> 0), so the GPU was
    strictly the harsher of the two.

    Why it went unnoticed: the parity fixture in
    tests/test_gpu_ecosystem.py fills EVERY cell with biomass, so a
    split always arrives in a cell that is viable on its own account
    and the suppression never fires. The discrepancy only shows up
    when destinations are empty.

81.2 The mirror
    New TensorEcosystem.suppress_splits(b_out, r_out, b_total,
    r_total), called from movement() after migration and BEFORE the
    reserve clip -- the reference's order exactly:
      * dest = b_total[:, :, self.neighbors] gathers the tentative
        destination total, aligned on the SOURCE cell; off-grid
        directions are set to +inf, i.e. treated as unblocked, so the
        migration emigration/immigration path (with its own top-k
        concentration) is untouched.
      * blocked = (b_out > 0) & (dest < split_thr), b_cancel/r_cancel
        added back to the source, then subtracted at the destination
        with exactly the settle loop's gather and the sign flipped.
      * clamp_min(0) for the float32 round-off on the +/- pair.
    New static tensors next to threshold: split_thr ([1, D, 1, 1], the
    same product per DM, broadcast over the direction axis) and
    split_thr_any (the reference's _dm_split_thr_any). Everything is
    fixed-shape with no data-dependent branch, so the tick still
    traces as one graph for compile / cuda-graph execution.

    The local reward (sections 79-80) needed one correction: a
    cancelled split stayed home, so movement(track=True) now moves
    b_cancel from b_out into b_stay before publishing the flow.
    Otherwise the destination that never received the biomass would be
    credited with the share and sum_c B = sum_c Q(t+1) would break.

81.3 Verification
    * tests/test_gpu_ecosystem.py: sparse_env / uniform_move helpers
      (one cell with 0.1 t, velocity 0.7, four directions -> 0.0175 t
      per destination, thr = 1.2 * 0.05 = 0.06) and
      test_subthreshold_splits_are_suppressed_like_the_reference:
      per-tick b/r parity over 4 ticks, plus an assertion that the
      suppression actually fires (compared against split_thr_any =
      False, the pre-fix code path). Measured on tick 0: 0.329494 t
      without vs. 0.414619 t with suppression, the reference giving
      0.414619 -- a fifth of the population, and now bit-identical.
    * tests/test_gpu_local_reward.py: test_tracking_follows_
      suppressed_splits (mass balance with the suppression firing),
      test_rollout_fitness_matches_the_reference_with_suppression
      (fitness parity vs ARSTrainer._evaluate_coevo with thresholds
      on) and the graph-trace test parametrised over both fixtures.
    * pytest tests/ -q -> 407 passed, 12 skipped (was 398/12).
    * Real project, 24x24, 40 ticks, random policies, suppression on
      vs off:
        gadoids       444.12 vs 443.99 t, 377 vs 372 occupied cells
        pelagic_fish 1237.08 vs 1237.01 t, 541 vs 540 cells
        porpoises       3.68 vs    3.68 t,  50 vs  50 cells
      Small in absolute terms -- mareld2 thresholds are 0.25 kg for
      the fish and 5 kg for porpoises -- but systematic, and it is the
      occupied-cell count that matters for --local_reward with
      --local_reward_norm sum.
    * train_gpu.py --local_reward, 1 generation, 12x12, device cpu:
      unchanged from 80.4 (4 ticks on a dense grid never trigger the
      suppression), so the flag's behaviour did not regress.

81.4 Still open
    * The GPU engine still has no equivalent of the reference's
      impact mortality; only the split suppression was mirrored here.
    * --migration on: unchanged, see 80.5.

82 --local_reward T1: does the occupied-cell count run away?
    The first of the evaluation experiments listed in 79.8/80.6. The
    correctness tests only say the source tracking is exact; they say
    nothing about whether the local reward behaves well AS a fitness
    function. T1 asks the single cheapest question: does the raw
    ratio-sum normalisation reward spreading the population thin
    instead of doing well locally?

82.1 Setup
    Three arms, same seed, same everything else, on the GPU path:
        sum_ratio      --local_reward_norm sum  --local_reward_metric ratio
                       --local_reward_theta 0      (the literal formulation)
        mean_log       --local_reward_norm mean --local_reward_metric log
                       --local_reward_theta 0      (the defaults)
        global_theta1  --local_reward_norm mean --local_reward_metric log
                       --local_reward_theta 1      (= the global reward by
                                                    construction, 79.6)
    Command per arm (seeds 1 and 2):
        python3 train_gpu.py --project mareld2.yaml --grid 24x24 \
            --profile info --generations 20 --ticks 100 --seed <s> \
            --local_reward <arm flags> --output results/t1_<arm>
    --profile info fixes temperature at 1.0 and zeroes entropy_coef and
    argmax_penalty, so reward_mean is the raw ecological reward. 400
    updates per arm, ~0.93 s/update on the local GPU, about 6 min each.
    local_occupancy is already logged per update in training.jsonl
    (lib/gpu/trainer.py, mean occupied cells per tick and per DM), so no
    code change was needed.

82.2 Result: the spreading gradient is real
    Mean of the first three vs the last three generations, occupied
    cells out of 576, and mean biomass in tonnes. Both seeds agree.

    Occupied cells, porpoises (the only DM with headroom):
        sum_ratio       41.3 -> 51.4   and   45.9 -> 49.8
        mean_log        43.9 -> 38.5   and   48.0 -> 39.1
        global_theta1   43.2 -> 35.5   and   49.0 -> 11.9
    Occupied cells, gadoids:
        sum_ratio      568.6 -> 569.3  and  568.6 -> 569.4
        mean_log       507.2 -> 470.0  and  511.8 -> 473.8
        global_theta1  508.0 -> 482.6  and  514.6 -> 477.4
    Porpoise biomass over the same window:
        sum_ratio       3.85 -> 3.55   and   4.28 -> 3.96
        mean_log        4.59 -> 5.23   and   4.93 -> 5.27
        global_theta1   4.98 -> 5.60   and   5.37 -> 6.45

    So the sum arm moves the occupancy UP while the biomass of the same
    group moves DOWN, and it is the only arm that keeps gadoids pinned
    at near-full occupancy. The other two arms let the population
    contract and end with 40-80 percent more porpoise biomass. That is
    exactly the trade predicted in 79.3: every extra cell adds about
    1.0 to the sum, so thinning out pays even when it costs energy.
    zooplankton sits at 576/576 in every arm and pelagic_fish at ~573,
    so those two have no room to show the effect; porpoises, with 35-50
    of 576 cells, is the sensitive group (as already seen in 80.4).

82.3 Conclusions
    * --local_reward_norm sum with theta=0 must not be used as a
      fitness function. It optimises spatial extent, not local
      performance. Keep it as the diagnostic that reproduces the
      literal B/A formulation, nothing more.
    * The defaults (log + mean, theta=0) do not show the runaway: N_occ
      falls, biomass rises, and it stays between sum_ratio and the
      global arm. Defaults confirmed as the right choice.
    * theta=1 + norm mean is the strongest contraction of the three and
      gave the single most extreme case (porpoises down to 11.9 cells
      on seed 2). It behaves like the global reward, which is the point
      of the arm, and it is also the "survive in one corner" risk from
      79.3 showing up in the baseline rather than in the local reward.
    * T2 (theta=0 vs theta=1 on reward-independent metrics, 3+ seeds)
      is now the meaningful next experiment; run it with norm mean only.
      T4 (the cornered-start degeneration test) has become more
      interesting than it looked, since contraction turned out to be
      the baseline's failure mode, not the local reward's.

83 --local_reward T2: is it better than the global reward?
    The main experiment from 79.8/82.3. T1 only ruled out one
    normalisation; it could not say whether the local reward is BETTER,
    because the arms optimise different scalars and their reward curves
    are not comparable. T2 trains the arms and then scores them on
    reward-independent metrics in a shared evaluation rollout.

83.1 Setup: ab_test_local_reward.py
    New script in the repo root, same spirit as ab_test_literature.py
    but built around training rather than pure simulation. Two stages:

        python3 ab_test_local_reward.py --stage train
        python3 ab_test_local_reward.py --stage eval

    train delegates to train_gpu.py, one subprocess per (arm, seed),
    and skips a run that already has trainer.pth, so the script is
    restartable. eval loads results/t2_<arm>_s<seed>/policy_*.pth via
    TensorARSTrainer.import_policies (which also restores obs_stats)
    and runs its own RolloutRunner with local_reward=None, i.e. no arm
    is scored with its own fitness function.

    Four arms, all with --local_reward_norm mean --local_reward_metric
    log, seeds 1/2/3, 24x24, --profile info, 20 generations, 100 ticks
    (400 updates, ~6.5 min per run on the local GPU, 12 runs):
        theta0    w_c = 1          the local reward
        theta05   w_c = A_c**0.5   halfway
        theta1    w_c = A_c        = the global reward (79.6)
        global    no flag at all   sanity: must match theta1

    Evaluation: 4 worlds from WorldSpawner keyed on --eval-seed 777
    only, so every arm and seed sees bit-identical initial biomass,
    reserves and phases; 300 ticks; temperature 1.0. Metrics per FG,
    averaged over worlds and then over seeds: final/mean/min biomass,
    occupied cells (same viability bar as the extinction sweep),
    normalised Shannon entropy over cells, swing (coefficient of
    variation over the second half, i.e. the boom-bust amplitude of
    POPULATION_STABILITY.md), and the fraction of worlds ending below
    a thousandth of their start. Raw numbers in
    results/t2_local_reward.json.

83.2 Result: the total is unchanged, the DISTRIBUTION is not
    Total end-of-rollout DM energy (biomass * energy_content), which
    is what the global reward maximises:
        theta0   39.30 M MJ
        theta05  38.16 M MJ
        theta1   38.62 M MJ
        global   38.86 M MJ
    A 3 percent spread over a factor-of-four change in the weighting
    exponent. The arms do not make the system richer or poorer; they
    move energy between groups.

    Final biomass in tonnes, mean +- sd over the three seeds:
                    gadoids     pelagic_fish   porpoises    zooplankton
        theta0      611 +- 6     1472 +- 19    1.14 +- 0.18  5531 +- 88
        theta05     588 +- 7     1580 +- 55    0.98 +- 0.66  5125 +- 133
        theta1      590 +- 8     1755 +- 113   0.43 +- 0.33  4935 +- 53
        global      574 +- 3     1806 +- 50    0.58 +- 0.29  4923 +- 77

    * Sanity holds: theta1 and global agree within the seed noise on
      every group. The single-knob claim in 79.6 is confirmed on
      trained policies, not just in a unit test.
    * theta0 shifts energy DOWN the food chain: zooplankton +12 percent
      and gadoids +6 percent against the global arm, pelagic_fish -18
      percent. The ordering is monotone in theta for all three, so it
      is the weighting that drives it, not seed noise.
    * Porpoises, the group with spatial headroom (5-12 of 576 cells),
      is the clearest single win: theta0 beats every other arm in every
      seed (0.91/1.14/1.36 t against global 0.22/0.93/0.59 and theta1
      0.40/0.04/0.86). This is the group the global reward ignores
      because it contributes least to E_total, which is precisely the
      motivation for the local reward in 79.

83.3 The price, and why there is no clean winner yet
    * Variability. theta0 has the highest swing for pelagic_fish
      (0.043 vs 0.015) and zooplankton (0.055 vs 0.056, a tie), but the
      lowest for porpoises (0.35 vs 0.42 global, 0.71 theta1). So the
      local reward stabilises the sensitive group and destabilises the
      large ones.
    * Porpoises go bimodal. theta0 has the highest mean biomass AND the
      highest loss rate (extinct fraction per seed 0.5/0.25/0.5 of four
      worlds, against 0.25/0.25/0.25 for global). It does very well
      where it survives and still loses whole worlds. theta05 is the
      only arm that drops to zero losses on a seed (0.25/0.25/0.0).
    * Spatial spread. theta0 keeps pelagic_fish at 576/576 and the
      highest entropy for pelagic_fish and zooplankton, but the LOWEST
      final occupancy for gadoids (467 vs theta1's 522) -- with norm
      mean there is no spreading gradient left (82.2), so this is a
      genuine allocation choice, not the T1 artefact.
    * Three seeds and four worlds is thin for porpoises, where the
      between-seed sd is of the same size as the mean. The group-level
      orderings (gadoids, pelagic_fish, zooplankton) are monotone in
      theta and consistent across seeds; the porpoise loss rate is not.

83.4 Conclusions
    * The local reward is not a free improvement and not a regression.
      It is a different allocation: it trades pelagic_fish biomass for
      zooplankton, gadoids and -- most of all -- porpoises, at constant
      total energy. Whether that is better is an ecological question
      about what the Mareld case is supposed to represent, not a
      question the fitness curve can answer.
    * If the scenario question concerns the groups the global reward
      cannot see (porpoises, seals, seabirds -- the ones with small
      energy content totals and few occupied cells), theta=0 is the
      right choice and the gain is large relative to the seed noise.
    * theta=0.5 is the interesting middle: closest to theta0 on
      porpoise biomass while keeping the lowest porpoise loss rate and
      most of pelagic_fish. Worth its own arc if the bimodality in 83.3
      turns out to matter.
    * Next, in order: (a) repeat the porpoise arms with more seeds and
      more evaluation worlds, since that is the only claim the data
      does not settle; (b) T4, the cornered-start test, which 82.3
      already reframed as a baseline failure mode; (c) T5/T6
      (ratio vs log, clip sensitivity) as robustness checks.

84 --local_reward: THE FIXED DENOMINATOR (norm grid) AND A SYMMETRIC CLIP
    Applied change, following the three long runs pzbpgp248nsrpfix16/17/18
    (16x16, migration on, mortality off, --profile info, 2x48 tanh,
    lr 0.03, sigma 0.05, 800-1400 updates, identical except
    --local_reward_theta 0 / 0.5 / 1). Those runs showed the defaults
    (metric log + norm mean + theta 0) to be exploitable, in the exact
    mirror image of the T1 failure in section 82.

84.1 The diagnosis: BOTH normalisations have a cell-count gradient
    * norm sum (82): a neutral cell is worth 1.0 under the raw ratio, so
      every extra occupied cell raises the sum. The policy spreads thin,
      just above the extinction threshold.
    * norm mean (these runs): the denominator is the weight of the cells
      that were active AT THE START of the tick. A cell that is zeroed
      pays log(clip_lo) once and then disappears from the denominator
      for every later tick. Killing your worst cells raises the mean
      forever after. Measured correlation between local_occupancy and
      reward over training:
          theta=0     gadoids -0.88   pelagic_fish -0.82
          theta=0.5   gadoids -0.72   pelagic_fish -0.50
          theta=1     gadoids -0.08   pelagic_fish -0.16
      Occupancy fell 256->215 and 253->195 in the theta=0 arm while its
      reward rose by a factor ~20 and the stock fell to 15 percent of
      the start (gadoids 307->56 t, pelagic_fish 918->136 t). Fitness
      and ecology pointed in opposite directions.
    * Second effect, same runs: the loss budget went to 88-100 percent
      starvation in the theta=0 arm (eat 17-23 percent, rest 23-26
      percent) against 11 percent starvation and 80 percent eat at
      theta=1. Per tick a starving cell loses at most
      starve_rate * u = 0.012 * 0.5 = 0.006, i.e. log(B/A) ~ -0.006,
      while a heavily grazed cell clips at log(0.2) = -1.61 -- a factor
      ~270. A(c,t) rebases every tick, so you pay for SHRINKING, never
      for BEING small: a slow bleed is almost invisible to B/A while
      predation is maximally visible. The global reward does not have
      this problem because log(E(t)/E(0)) is measured against the start.

84.2 The fix: norm grid
    A third normalisation, --local_reward_norm grid, divides the
    weighted sum by the CONSTANT number of cells in the grid (H*W on the
    reference path, model.C on the tensor path) instead of by the weight
    of the active cells:
        f_X = mean_t ( (1/(H*W)) * sum_c clip(log(B_c(t+1)/A_c(t))) )
    With metric log a neutral cell contributes exactly 0, so neither
    adding nor dropping cells moves the fitness -- the cell count is
    orthogonal to fitness by construction, which neither sum (neutral
    cell = 1.0) nor mean (shrinking denominator) achieves. The divisor
    is a pure scale factor that only makes values comparable between
    grid sizes; it changes no gradient. And because there is no
    per-tick renormalisation, sum_t log(B/A) telescopes onto
    log(end/start) for a surviving cell line, so a slow bleed is priced
    exactly as the global reward prices it.

84.3 The fix: a symmetric clip
    The default clip moved from [0.2, 2.0] to [0.2, 5.0], i.e.
    log(5) = -log(0.2) = 1.609. The old range priced a halving 2.3x
    higher than a doubling, on top of starvation already being
    underpriced (84.1), which pushed the policy towards hiding rather
    than feeding. DEFAULT_CLIP in source_tracking.py is now the single
    source of truth for the config default, the CLI default and the
    local_reward_options fallback. Runs started before this section used
    [0.2, 2.0]; pass --local_reward_clip 0.2 2.0 to reproduce them.

84.4 Where it is wired in
    * lib/environments/ecosystem_env/source_tracking.py: NORMS gained
      "grid", DEFAULT_CLIP added, the grid branch in end_tick, and the
      --local_reward_norm choices now come from NORMS so the CPU and GPU
      parsers cannot drift (the flags are already shared, see 80.3).
    * lib/gpu/rollout.py::_accumulate_local_reward: the same branch,
      dividing by float(self.model.C). A static python scalar, so the
      tick still traces as one fixed-shape graph.
    * No banner changes were needed: train.py and train_gpu.py both
      print config.norm and the clip range verbatim.

84.5 Verification
    * python3 -m pytest tests/ -q -> 414 passed, 12 skipped (was
      407/12). New in tests/test_local_reward.py: grid == sum/(H*W)
      exactly; grid + log scores a one-cell and a full grid identically
      (0.0 both), which is the cell-count blindness claim; the default
      clip satisfies clip_lo * clip_hi == 1. tests/test_gpu_local_reward
      .py gained LocalRewardConfig(metric="log", norm="grid") to CONFIGS,
      so the whole parity matrix (mass balance, fitness against
      ARSTrainer._evaluate_coevo, graph tracing) covers it and also
      asserts that H*W and model.C agree.
    * Smoke runs: train_gpu.py --local_reward --local_reward_norm grid
      (12x12, cpu, 1 generation) and train.py --local_reward
      --local_reward_norm grid (1 generation, pelagic_fish).

84.6 The falsifiable prediction this exists to test
    Re-run the pzbpgp248nsrpfix16 command with
    --local_reward_norm grid (defaults otherwise: metric log, theta 0,
    clip 0.2 5.0). If the diagnosis in 84.1 is right, then against arm
    16 we must see: corr(local_occupancy, reward) -> ~0 (as in the
    theta=1 arm), the eat share back towards the theta=1 level, and the
    starvation share of the loss budget down from 88-100 percent. If
    occupancy still collapses, the cause is elsewhere and the
    normalisation was not the problem.
    Not addressed here, and independent of the reward: there is no
    crowding cost for decision makers (Holling II is purely prey
    dependent), so packing is free and aggregation is always optimal.
    That is the mechanism behind the vertical banding, and no reward
    formula removes it.

85 --local_reward: THE DIAGNOSIS OF 84.6, RUN AND SCORED
    The prediction in 84.6 was tested with the pzbpgp248nsrpfix16
    command, only --local_reward_norm grid added (defaults otherwise:
    metric log, theta 0, clip 0.2 5.0), run as results/lrgrid16:
        python3 train_gpu.py --project mareld2.yaml --run-name lrgrid16
            --migration on --mortality off --localreward
            --local_reward_norm grid --profile info --generations 9
            --iter-per-gen 50 --n_eval_ticks 1000 --n_deltas 16
            --grid 16x16 --worlds_refresh iteration
            --policynetwork 2 48 tanh --lr 0.03 --sigma 0.05
    450 updates, the same count the three older arms are truncated to
    so the comparison is at equal optimisation budget. Seed 0 in all
    four, no --visual (4.1-4.8 s/update, 35 min).

85.1 Verdict: two of the three predictions hold, one does not
    Loss budget and action mix, measured by replaying the saved
    policies in the REFERENCE engine (tools/probes/loss_budget.py,
    400 ticks, one fixed world, reward independent):
        arm            gadoids B / occ   pelagic B / occ   pf starve%  pf eat
        grid  th0      179.2 / 223       740.1 / 255           59       0.44
        mean  th0 (16) 139.0 / 203       209.1 / 220           92       0.16
        mean  th0.5(18)181.3 / 184       787.8 / 222           71       0.46
        mean  th1 (17) 217.8 / 217      1044.0 / 224            7       0.49
    * The eat share is back: pelagic_fish 0.16 -> 0.44, essentially the
      theta=1 level (0.49). CONFIRMED.
    * The starvation share fell 92 -> 59 percent for pelagic_fish, and
      its rest share 0.34 -> 0.06. CONFIRMED, but only halfway: the
      theta=1 arm sits at 7 percent. Gadoids are still at 100 percent
      starvation in every local arm.
    * The stock follows: pelagic_fish 209 -> 740 t, a factor 3.5, and
      occupancy 220 -> 255 of 256. grid at theta=0 lands roughly where
      mean at theta=0.5 lands, i.e. it buys about half the distance to
      the global reward without giving up locality.

85.2 What did NOT happen: corr(occupancy, reward) did not go to zero
    From the training logs (tools/probes/local_reward_log.py, first 450
    updates of each run), correlation between local_occupancy and
    reward, plus occupancy and mean biomass over the first/last fifth:
        arm            gadoids corr  occ         pelagic corr  occ
        grid  th0      -0.84  255->233           -0.43  256->236
        mean  th0 (16) -0.91  256->220           -0.86  255->215
        mean  th0.5(18)-0.45  256->248           +0.12  256->256
        mean  th1 (17) -0.13  256->256           +0.34  256->256
    Better than arm 16 on both groups, but nowhere near the theta=1
    level. So the shrinking denominator was NOT the whole mechanism.
    The residue is structural and independent of the normalisation: a
    cell that is zeroed pays log(clip_lo) ONCE and then stops producing
    terms altogether. A cell kept alive while slowly bleeding keeps
    producing negative terms for the rest of the rollout. Killing a bad
    cell is therefore still cheaper than nursing it, because A(c,t)
    rebases every tick and there is no term for a cell that no longer
    exists. The telescoping argument in 84.2 only holds for a SURVIVING
    cell line; death truncates the sum instead of charging it.
    A reward that removes this would have to keep dead cells in the
    term set (an absorbing per-tick penalty), or measure against a
    fixed A(c,0) rather than rebasing. Neither is implemented.

85.3 The banding got WORSE, not better
    Replay of the final policies in one fixed world, 300 ticks
    (tools/probes/axis_collapse.py), against the same untrained control:
        arm            gadoids aniso/nyq_x   pelagic aniso/nyq_x   pf total
        untrained          3.07 / 0.02           2.25 / 0.00        283 t
        grid  th0         24.31 / 0.56          22.84 / 0.96        639 t
        mean  th0 (16)     4.98 / 0.01           9.98 / 0.26        299 t
    Here aniso is var(column means)/var(row means) and nyq_x the share
    of the column profile's spectral power at the 2-cell wavelength.
    The grid arm is both far more productive and far more striped: with
    nyq_x = 0.96 the pelagic field is essentially a pure Nyquist comb
    along x. This is consistent with, and strengthens, the reading in
    84.6: the bands are an AGGREGATION phenomenon, not a normalisation
    artefact. Nothing in the tick charges for packing (Holling II is
    purely prey dependent, no predator interference and no per-cell
    carrying capacity for decision makers), so once the reward stops
    punishing the policy for feeding, it aggregates harder. No reward
    formula removes this; a crowding term in predation() is the fix.

85.4 Practical conclusion
    * Keep --local_reward_norm grid as the recommended setting. It is
      the only cell-count neutral normalisation and it removes most of
      the hide-and-starve behaviour, which is the failure mode that
      made the defaults unusable.
    * Do not claim it removes the survivorship bias: corr is -0.84 and
      -0.43, not 0. 85.2 explains why, and the fix is a different one.
    * The next real step is mechanical, not a reward change: a crowding
      cost for decision makers. Until then any local reward will find
      the Nyquist comb, and spatial metrics (N_occ, entropy) measure
      the grid rather than the ecology.
    * porpoises remain untestable at 16x16: they start at 2.84 t and
      are gone in the first iteration in all four arms.

85.5 Probes kept
    tools/probes/local_reward_log.py  training.jsonl: corr(occupancy,
        reward), occupancy/reward/biomass over the first and last fifth,
        action mix. Takes run names, --updates truncates to a common
        budget.
    tools/probes/loss_budget.py       replays saved policies in the
        reference engine and reports the loss budget (starvation vs
        predation) and action mix, which training.jsonl does not carry.
    tools/probes/axis_collapse.py     anisotropy and Nyquist share of
        the biomass field, trained against an untrained control in the
        same world.

================================================================
86. PREDATOR INTERFERENCE (BEDDINGTON-DEANGELIS): THE CROWDING
    TERM THAT REMOVES THE BANDS
================================================================

86.1 Why the bands existed at all
    Sections 84-85 chased the vertical 2-cell bands through the reward
    function and concluded the cause was not there. It was in the
    functional response. interactions.holling_a_eff was purely PREY
    dependent:

        f(B_prey) = a*B_prey / (1 + a*h*B_prey)

    and predation.py multiplies it by the predator's own biomass:

        demand = B_pred * pi_eat * f(B_prey) * hunger

    so demand is EXACTLY linear in B_pred. 100 t of gadoids in one cell
    get ten times the food of 10 t in the same cell. Nothing else in
    the tick charges for packing either: max_carrying_capacity is only
    read for non decision makers, and DM growth is pure energy
    bookkeeping (q = s - u). Packing was therefore free at every
    density, aggregation was always weakly optimal, and the resulting
    positive density feedback is anti-diffusive. The fastest growing
    mode of an anti-diffusive operator on a grid is the shortest
    wavelength the grid can carry, i.e. 2 cells - measured as nyq_x =
    0.96 in 85.3. The bands were the grid's Nyquist mode, not ecology.

86.2 The term
        f(B_prey, B_pred) = a*B_prey / (1 + a*h*B_prey + w_X*B_X(c))

    w_X is the new per-FG parameter `interference` [1/ton], read in
    lib/world/functional_group.py, cached per DM as env.dm_interference
    (shape (N_dm,1,1,1), dm_ids order) with the env._has_interference
    switch in state.py, and applied in predation.apply_predation as
    I = w_X * B_X(c) broadcast over the prey axis. Intraspecific only:
    it is the standard form and it keeps the rows of the predation
    tensor independent.

    Properties that made this the right shape:
    * w = 0 is bit-identical to the old response (x + 0.0 == x), so the
      default keeps every existing .pth valid. Both engines and the
      whole test suite were verified unchanged at w = 0.
    * The Holling contract of tests/test_holling_response.py survives:
      zero at zero prey, strictly increasing in prey, bounded by 1/h.
      Interference lowers the curve, it does not deform it.
    * Total intake in a cell now tends to a*B_prey/w as B_pred -> inf
      instead of growing without bound. That finite ceiling is exactly
      the queue at the dinner table, and it is what gives aggregation a
      finite optimum instead of a corner solution.
    * It is cellwise and multiplicative, so source_tracking.py and the
      --local_reward mass balance identity are untouched.
    * The unsaturated branch (h = 0, no Holling) does NOT go through
      holling_a_eff, so predation.py divides the bare intake rate by
      (1 + I) there. Easy branch to miss; tests cover it.

    Mirrored in lib/gpu/ecosystem.py as the same one-line denominator
    change, fixed-shape, so the tick still traces as a graph.

86.3 Calibration
    The density scale was read off the banded run rather than guessed:
    replay results/lrgrid16 and compare, over the cells the predator
    actually occupies, the saturation term sum_j a*h*B_prey_j at the
    median cell against the 90th percentile of the predator's own
    per-cell biomass.

        FG             B_pred p50   p90     sat p50    sat/p90
        gadoids            0.017   1.95        6.3        3.2
        pelagic_fish       0.60    6.5        15.0        2.3

    sat/p90 is the w that makes interference as strong as prey
    saturation at the observed peak density. Two strengths were then
    trained (86.4); the weaker one won, so the library ships with one
    third of it:

        gadoids       interference: 1.0
        pelagic_fish  interference: 0.7
        zooplankton   0.0  (p90/p50 = 1.4, it does not aggregate; a
                            term here would be a uniform rescaling of
                            grazing, not a spatial mechanism)
        porpoises     0.0  (a handful of cells at <1 t; already the
                            group most at risk of extinction)

86.4 The control run - the bands are gone
    Exactly the pzbpgp248nsrpfix16 command plus --local_reward_norm
    grid, i.e. identical to results/lrgrid16 in every respect except
    the interference parameter. 450 updates each, 16x16, --profile
    info, migration on, mortality off, 1000 ticks, 16 deltas, 3 worlds,
    2x48 tanh, lr 0.03, sigma 0.05.

      python3 train_gpu.py --project mareld2.yaml --run-name interf16 \
        --migration on --mortality off --generations 9 --local_reward \
        --local_reward_norm grid --profile info --iter-per-gen 50 \
        --n_eval_ticks 1000 --n_deltas 16 --grid 16x16 \
        --worlds_refresh iteration --policynetwork 2 48 tanh \
        --lr 0.03 --sigma 0.05

    tools/probes/axis_collapse.py, 300 ticks, against an untrained
    control in the same world (aniso = var(column means)/var(row
    means), nyq_x = share of the column profile's spectral power at the
    2-cell wavelength):

        arm                      gadoids         pelagic_fish
                              aniso   nyq_x     aniso   nyq_x
        untrained              1.8-2.8  0.03     2.5-2.7  0.00
        lrgrid16   w = 0      19.23    0.53     18.66    0.97
        interf16   w = 3/2     0.32    0.00      0.92    0.02
        interf16_l w = 1/0.7   0.39    0.07      1.33    0.01

    Unambiguous: with the term on, both groups end up LESS anisotropic
    than an untrained policy, and the Nyquist comb is gone. The
    directional mix confirms the mechanism rather than just the
    symptom - the trained east/west dominance of 85.3 (N/E/S/W =
    0.003/0.346/0.162/0.203) is replaced by a balanced mix
    (0.027/0.101/0.105/0.141).

    The bands were therefore an aggregation phenomenon all along, and
    no reward formula could have removed them. Note also that replaying
    the OLD banded policies under the new mechanics only drops aniso
    from 24.3 to 19.2: the term does not merely smear the field, the
    policy has to re-optimise against it. It is a change of incentive,
    not a diffusion filter.

86.5 What it costs, and why the weaker w won
    training.jsonl, first vs last fifth of 450 updates
    (tools/probes/local_reward_log.py):

        arm          fg             eat   rest  move   bio0   bio1
        w = 0        gadoids       0.18  0.20  0.61   274    215
                     pelagic       0.59  0.04  0.37   702    467
        w = 1/0.7    gadoids       0.51  0.01  0.48   256    148
                     pelagic       0.66  0.01  0.33   745    277
        w = 3/2      gadoids       0.54  0.01  0.46   232    127
                     pelagic       0.62  0.10  0.28   729    264

    Two things to read here. First, the policy stops wandering and
    starts feeding (gadoids eat 0.18 -> 0.51, move 0.61 -> 0.48):
    with a crowding cost, chasing the winning column no longer pays,
    so the fish eats where it stands. Second, biomass is LOWER. That is
    not a bug - interference is a real reduction in intake, and the
    w = 0 arm's higher biomass was bought by the degenerate packing we
    set out to remove. But it is a cost, and the weaker calibration
    pays less of it for the same result (gadoids 148 vs 127, pelagic
    277 vs 264) while removing the bands just as completely. Hence
    1.0 / 0.7 in the library.

    Loss budget in the reference engine (tools/probes/loss_budget.py,
    400 ticks, fixed world) still shows 95-100 percent starvation for
    both fish groups. So the crowding term does NOT fix the starvation
    story of 85.2 - it is a different problem with a different cause,
    and if anything a strong w makes it worse by lowering intake.
    corr(local_occupancy, reward) is likewise unchanged (-0.79/-0.71
    against -0.84/-0.43): the survivorship bias explained in 85.2 is
    orthogonal to crowding, as expected.

86.6 Tests
    tests/test_interference.py (11 cases): w = 0 bit-identical in
    holling_a_eff and over a live predation step; the Holling shape
    contract preserved under w for both Type II and Type III; total
    cell intake linear in B_pred without the term and converging to
    a*B_prey/w with it; per-unit intake monotonically diminishing; the
    term negligible at sparse density and exactly halving at the
    density where interference equals saturation; the unsaturated
    h = 0 branch; and the live library's coefficients wired through to
    dm_interference in dm_ids order.
    tests/test_gpu_ecosystem.py: test_interference_matches_reference,
    per-tick CPU/GPU parity for both handling = 0 and 0.2, asserting
    the GPU actually took the interference path.
    Suite: 432 passed, 12 skipped (was 414/12).

86.7 What this does and does not settle
    Settled: the bands, and with them the premise of 85.4. N_occ and
    spatial entropy now measure ecology rather than the grid's Nyquist
    frequency, which is what the whole --local_reward evaluation rests
    on. Sections 82-85 should be re-read with that in mind: every
    spatial number in them was taken in a banded regime.
    Not settled: the survivorship bias (85.2), the starvation-heavy
    loss budget, impact mortality on the GPU engine, and porpoises,
    which are still gone in the first iteration at 16x16 in every arm.
    Open calibration question: only {0, w/3, w} were swept. Whether an
    even weaker term still clears the bands is untested, and the
    biomass cost suggests it is worth one more run.
87 THE GLOBAL-REWARD CONTROL RUN (pzbpgp248nsrpfix20)
    The measurement 86.4 was missing: interference lives in the tick
    (predation.py / lib/gpu/ecosystem.py::predation), not in the reward
    path, so the bands should disappear with the GLOBAL reward too.
    Expected, but until this run untested.

      __NV_PRIME_RENDER_OFFLOAD=1 __GLX_VENDOR_LIBRARY_NAME=nvidia \
      python3 train_gpu.py --project mareld2.yaml \
        --run-name pzbpgp248nsrpfix20 --migration on --mortality off \
        --generations inf --profile info --iter-per-gen 50 \
        --n_eval_ticks 2500 --n_deltas 16 --grid 16x16 \
        --worlds_refresh iteration --policynetwork 2 48 tanh \
        --lr 0.03 --sigma 0.05 --visual

    2876 updates, 17.2 h, cuda-graph, 3 worlds. No --local_reward.

87.1 The bands are gone, and it is reward independent
    tools/probes/axis_collapse.py, 300 ticks, against an untrained
    control in the same fixed world:

        FG            arm        total   occ  aniso  nyq_x
        gadoids       untrained  136.9   256   2.83   0.02
        gadoids       trained    178.1   254   0.32   0.18
        pelagic_fish  untrained  253.2   256   2.46   0.00
        pelagic_fish  trained    806.4   256   3.19   0.00

    Compare the banded regime (85.3): aniso 19-23 and nyq_x 0.96-0.97.
    Here anisotropy is at or below the untrained control and the
    Nyquist comb is in the noise. The east/west collapse of arm 16
    (N/E/S/W = 0.003/0.346/0.162/0.203) is gone; the directions are
    symmetric. The mechanism is therefore in the functional response,
    exactly as 86.2 claimed, and does not depend on the fitness used.

87.2 Global reward + interference beats local reward + interference
    Loss budget in the reference engine (tools/probes/loss_budget.py,
    400 ticks, fixed world, reward independent):

        arm             FG            biomass  occ   loss  starve%  eat
        fix20 global    gadoids         211.4  240    0.1     96   0.88
        interf16_low    gadoids          65.5  226  117.8    100   0.44
        fix20 global    pelagic_fish    789.8  245  203.5     55   0.75
        interf16_low    pelagic_fish    228.0  136  669.5     95   0.74

    Three to four times the biomass, near-full occupancy, and
    starvation is no longer the whole loss budget. The hide-and-starve
    strategy of Section 85 is absent: gadoids lose 0.1 t over 400
    ticks. Caveat: this is not a perfectly controlled A/B - fix20 ran
    2500 ticks and 2876 updates against 1000/450 for interf16_low -
    but the direction and the size of the gap are far too large to be
    a horizon effect, and they point the same way as all of 84-85.

87.3 The practically most important finding: 15 of the 17 hours were
    wasted
    Reward, 50-update moving average, and the update at which 95
    percent of the total improvement is reached:

        FG             start    final   95% at update
        gadoids       -0.086   +0.420        22
        pelagic_fish  -0.927   -0.503        33
        zooplankton   +1.244   +1.508       358
        porpoises    -10.71   -12.58        517  (downwards)

    All of the fish learning happens inside the first ~30 updates.
    After that the trend is below the noise: the slope over the WHOLE
    run is 0.033 for gadoids against a per-update std of 0.047 and
    sigma_f 0.048. The decile means confirm it (gadoids 0.396 / 0.418
    / 0.347 / ... / 0.416, i.e. a random walk). At 19-22 s/update the
    remaining ~2500 updates (~15 h) produced nothing.
    Lesson for the budget: --iter-per-gen 50 --n_eval_ticks 2500
    --generations inf spends everything on updates. The horizon and
    the number of worlds (3) are where the money should go. Abort this
    kind of run at ~500 updates; the plateau is measurable in the log.

87.4 What the reward levels mean
    With --integral_reward the reward is mean_t log(E(t)/E(0)), i.e.
    measured against the SPAWN level:
        gadoids      +0.42  -> time-mean energy 1.5x the start
        pelagic_fish -0.50  -> 0.61x the start
        zooplankton  +1.51  -> 4.5x, biomass 8283 -> 9216 t
    pelagic_fish cannot hold its spawn level. The likely cause is that
    mareld2.yaml's spawn now sits above the equilibrium at
    interference: 0.7, not that learning failed. Worth recalibrating
    spawn against the new equilibrium - otherwise every reward
    measured against E(0) mostly reports "how far down to equilibrium".

87.5 What this setup cannot answer
    Porpoises: mean_biomass 0.008 t in the FIRST iteration, 0.02 t at
    the end, reward monotonically 10.7 -> -12.6 (pure extinction
    signal) and occ = 0 in the replay. At 16x16 they die before any
    policy matters, so every porpoise conclusion from fix16/17/18/20
    is empty. Needs 24x24 or 32x32, or a higher starting biomass.
    Spatial behaviour: the mean policy barely moves (sum N/E/S/W =
    0.027 gadoids, 0.031 pelagic in the replay) and entropy falls
    1.67 -> 0.38 and 1.40 -> 0.29 (--profile info zeroes entropy_coef).
    But mareld2.yaml has no environment maps, so a 16x16 grid is
    perfectly isotropic and there is nothing to move towards.
    "Stand still and eat" is the correct answer in that world; the
    conclusion is that the setup cannot evaluate spatial behaviour at
    all, not that the policy degenerated.

88. CLI FLAG --mortality_multiplier: ONE GLOBAL SCALE ON natural_mortality
--------------------------------------------------------------------------

88.1 Why
    The density-independent natural mortality is a per-FG constant in
    fgconfig/fg_library.yaml (zooplankton 0.0025, pelagic_fish 0.0005,
    gadoids 0.0002, porpoises 5e-05, ...). Sweeping it meant editing the
    library, which changes every run that loads it and is easy to forget
    to revert. --mortality_multiplier scales all of them at once from the
