        absent from the snapshot,
      * the button exists in BOTH train and inference mode, inside the
        status-bar rect, repaints when clicked, and its click never
        falls through to the heatmap underneath.

    Verified by deleting the rolling branch: 4 of the 7 pixel cases
    turn red. The other three are the default-behaviour case and the
    two robustness cases, which are correct to pass either way.
    Unregistering the button's hit-rect turns both button cases red.

    Suite: 660 passed, 15 skipped (was 651 after 99).

101 SIMULATED TIME IN THE STATUS BAR (2026-09-28)

101.1 What
    The status bar's first row now reads

        mode = train  tick = 2960  time = 2 y 40 d  gen = ...

    ADDED next to the tick, not instead of it: the tick is the engine's
    unit and the rest of the UI is in ticks (the probe-ticks slider, the
    playback bar), so removing it would have broken the correspondence
    between the two.

101.2 Where the conversion comes from
    project_metadata.tick_hours (section 97), never a hardcoded 6.
    LiveVisualizer takes a new ``tick_hours`` argument and resolves it
    with ``tick_time.resolve_tick_hours``; train.py, inference.py and
    api.py pass ``config_loader.project_tick_hours(...)`` from their
    project path. manual_play.py and simple_inference.py have no project
    file at all and keep the historical 6 h through the default, which
    is what resolve_tick_hours is for.

    The arithmetic is integer, on hours:

        days  = tick * tick_hours // 24
        years, rest = divmod(days, 365)

    Floats would put a whole year at "0 y 364 d" often enough to be
    noticed. The part of a day a sub-daily tick leaves over is DROPPED,
    not rounded: the tick counter beside it already carries the finer
    resolution, and a day that ticks over early reads as a bug.

101.3 The layout regression this caused, and the rule that fixes it
    The longer row 1 pushed the normalisation button (section 100) off
    the right edge of the window in the narrowest case - one FG, 905 px,
    a five-digit tick and gen/iter/T present: btn_end 951 > win 905.
    Section 100's placement rule (right aligned, falling back to just
    past the text) cannot hold both properties at once when the text
    outgrows the row.

    New rule: the button owns a FIXED slot at the right end and the TEXT
    is what yields. ``parts`` is already ordered by importance, so
    trailing fields are dropped until the rest fits - fps first, then
    grid, then solo, ... - and mode, tick and time are never dropped.
    In the 905 px case exactly one field (fps) goes; at 1136 px and up
    nothing does.

101.4 Tests
    tests/test_viz_sim_time.py, 16 cases:
      * 6 h ticks across 0, a part-day, a day, 40 days, a year exactly,
        one tick short of a year ("0 y 364 d") and two years and change,
      * the same tick number under 1 h, 12 h, 24 h and 168 h tick
        lengths, i.e. the project setting is what converts,
      * a missing tick length falls back to 6 h,
      * the rendered row 1 contains both "tick = N" and "time = ...",
        in that order (asserted by wrapping the font object - pygame's
        Font.render is read-only and cannot be monkeypatched),
      * an AST check that train.py, inference.py and api.py all pass
        tick_hours - a viewer told nothing renders 6 h for every
        project, silently and plausibly.

    tests/test_viz_normalisation.py +1: in the narrowest window the
    button stays inside the bar, the text stays out from under it, and
    mode/tick/time survive the trimming.

    Verified by deleting the code under test: ignoring the argument
    turns 7 of the 16 red, removing the trimming loop turns the layout
    case red.

    Suite: 677 passed, 15 skipped (was 660 after 100).

102 HOVER TOOLTIP DURING THE PERTURBATION ROLLOUTS (2026-09-28)

102.1 The report and what it actually was
    "The plot section does not respond while rollouts run" - narrowed
    by the user to: the hover tooltip appears during the PROBE rollout
    and nowhere else, i.e. not while the perturbation rollouts run.

    That distinction is the whole diagnosis. _draw_plot reads the live
    pointer position (pg.mouse.get_pos) and draws the tooltip as part
    of the frame, so the tooltip exists only while something repaints:

      probe rollout      update_biomass every tick -> _maybe_render
                         every tick -> tooltip follows the pointer.
      perturbation       nothing calls update_biomass. The trainer's
                         pump_callback runs pump_events every ~50 ms
                         and that is ALL that runs - and pump_events
                         repainted only when ``interacted`` was set,
                         which plain pointer movement never sets. It
                         is set by clicks, keys, wheel and the MOTION
                         branches that belong to an active DRAG.

    So the tooltip was not broken, it was never drawn: no repaint, no
    tooltip. Everything else in the window behaves the same way, but
    the tooltip is the only thing whose content depends on the pointer
    rather than on state, so it is the only thing you can notice.

    Ruled out first, by measurement rather than reading: clicks are not
    being lost. pump_events is called every tick during a probe
    (train.py:962) and every 50 ms during the pool wait
    (trainer._pool_map). On 60x60 with 6 FGs, update_biomass costs
    0.18 ms/tick, pump_events 0.01 ms and a full redraw 4.9 ms.

102.2 The fix
    A new last ``elif`` in the pump_events event chain: plain
    MOUSEMOTION sets a ``hover`` flag when the pointer is inside
    _plot_area_rect, or was at the previous motion (``self._plot_hover``)
    so the movement that LEAVES also repaints and the tooltip is erased
    rather than stranded.

    ``hover`` is tracked separately from ``interacted`` on purpose:
    interacted forces an immediate redraw (_last_frame_ts = 0), which is
    right for a click but wrong for a pointer that emits hundreds of
    motion events a second. Hover calls _maybe_render() instead and goes
    through the 30 fps cap. Being the LAST elif also means an active
    drag still claims the motion first and keeps its uncapped redraw -
    dragging a slider through a frame cap feels like it is sticking.

102.3 Tests
    tests/test_viz_hover.py, 6 cases:
      * movement over the plot repaints; movement elsewhere does not,
      * leaving repaints exactly once, and the movement after that
        costs nothing,
      * twenty motion events inside one frame budget produce one
        redraw,
      * a drag is not frame-capped,
      * end to end, in the reported situation: with NO update_biomass
        anywhere - only pump_events, which is exactly the perturbation
        phase - the pixels change when the pointer enters the plot,
        every changed pixel is inside _plot_area_rect, and the screen
        returns bit-identical when it leaves.

    The end-to-end case works headless because pygame's dummy driver
    honours mouse.set_pos, so get_pos returns it and the tooltip really
    is drawn.

    Verified by deleting the hover branch: 4 of the 6 turn red.

    Suite: 683 passed, 15 skipped (was 677 after 101).

102.4 Still open, deliberately
    In train mode the plot receives ONE point per probe, pushed after
    the tick loop ends (train.py:1093; the loop ends at 973), so during
    a rollout there is genuinely no new data to draw - the heatmaps
    animate next to a static curve. That is by construction: the x-axis
    is the global ARS step. Whether a within-rollout view is wanted
    (most cheaply as a separate tab fed from the per-tick totals
    update_biomass already computes, cleared per probe, which is what
    inference mode does) was raised and the user deferred it.

103 TICK LENGTH NARROWED TO 1-6 HOURS (2026-09-28)

103.1 What changed and where
    USER DECISION 2026-09-28: the configurable tick length is 1-6 h,
    not the 1-168 h section 97 shipped.

    The request was phrased as a change to fgconfig, but the FG editor
    already reads MIN_TICK_HOURS / MAX_TICK_HOURS from
    lib/world/tick_time.py for both its label and its live validation -
    that is what the single definition point is for. So the change is
    one constant there, and the GUI, resolve_tick_hours and every tool
    that converts ticks to real time follow from it. fgconfig.py itself
    is untouched.

    Why 6 is the ceiling: it is the length every number in
    fg_library.yaml is calibrated at. Past a quarter day a tick stops
    resolving the within-day structure the decision makers are
    calibrated against, and the rescale carries every parameter further
    from what was actually measured. The old comment justified 168 h as
    "the coarsest that still resolves the seasonal signal", which is
    true of the seasonal term and beside the point for the rest.

103.2 Consequence: tick_label lost its whole-days branch
    tick_label resolves its argument first, so with the ceiling at 6 no
    input can reach the branch that rendered 24 h as "1 d" and 168 h as
    "7 d" - tick_label(168) now returns "6 h", because 168 resolves to
    the default. A branch no input can take reads as support that is
    not there, so it is gone and the function is one line. Restoring it
    is part of widening the range again; the module comment says so.

103.3 Out-of-range values are still silent
    resolve_tick_hours has always fallen back to DEFAULT_TICK_HOURS for
    anything out of range, and that is unchanged. A project file
    carrying tick_hours: 12 therefore now runs at 6 h rather than being
    refused. Nothing in the repo is affected - mareld2.yaml is the only
    project file with the key and it is 6 - but a project written
    outside it would change behaviour without saying so. The FG editor
    is where the value is entered and it rejects out-of-range input
    loudly (verified below), so this only bites a hand-edited file.

103.4 Tests
    tests/test_tick_time.py: every test point above 6 h moved inside
    the range, keeping the property each one asserts:
      * resolution and label cases,
      * the round-trip pairs, now (6,1) (1,6) (6,3) (3,6) (2,5),
      * flux/loss/growth/period dimensional cases rebuilt around 3->6
        and 1->6 rather than 6->12 and 6->168,
      * the clamp case: zooplankton intake 0.25/1 h cannot be 6x that
        in a 6 h tick (it was 0.25/6 h and a 7 d tick),
      * the two real-time invariance properties now sweep 1..6,
      * the loader and viability cases.
    New: test_the_range_is_one_to_six_hours pins (MIN, MAX) == (1, 6)
    and that 7, 8, 12, 18, 24, 48 and 168 all resolve to the default.

    A side effect worth noting: test_round_trip_returns_the_original
    used to SKIP three cases because the round trip went through a
    clamp at 168 h. Inside 1-6 nothing clamps, so those three now
    actually assert. tests/test_tick_time.py went 78 -> 88 cases with
    0 skips.

    tests/test_viz_sim_time.py: the 12 h, 24 h and 168 h conversion
    cases moved to 2 h, 3 h and 6 h. They were asserting that the
    project setting is what converts, which is unchanged.

    Headless xvfb GUI run against SCRATCH copies, 12 checks: the label
    reads "Tick Length (hours, 1-6)"; live validation accepts 1, 3, 6
    and rejects 0, 7, 12, 168; Apply on 12 raises "Invalid Tick Length",
    leaves the library byte-identical and commits nothing; Apply on 3
    still rescales, stamps library_metadata.tick_hours and commits. The
    real fg_library.yaml was never touched.

    Suite: 693 passed, 12 skipped (was 683/15 after 102).

103.5 Docs
    AGENTS.md said "range 1-168" in two places; both now say 1-6 and
    point at this section alongside 97.

104 MOVEMENT_SPEED WAS RESCALED AS A SURVIVAL PROBABILITY (2026-09-28)

104.1 The report
    User set the tick length to 1 h in the FG editor and observed that
    movement_speed was NOT rescaled to 1/6 cell per tick for porpoises
    and pelagic_fish. Reproduced exactly:

        fg               v@6h   6->1 h    expected
        pelagic_fish    1.000   1.0000     0.1667
        porpoises       1.000   1.0000     0.1667
        seals           1.000   1.0000     0.1667
        seabirds        1.000   1.0000     0.1667
        gadoids         0.250   0.0468     0.0417
        zooplankton     0.020   0.0034     0.0033

    Two failures in one: the four FGs at v = 1.0 did not move at all,
    and the two that did moved too far.

104.2 Cause
    Section 97.5 filed movement_speed under "loss" - the class for a
    per-tick fraction REMOVED from a stock, a survival probability in
    disguise, which compounds as 1 - (1 - r)^k. It is in that class
    because it is a fraction in [0, 1], which is not the same thing.

    What the engine does with it (movement.apply_movement):

        b_out  = moving_biomass * v     # reaches the neighbour cell
        b_keep = moving_biomass - b_out # stays in the source cell

    so the moving cohort's centre of mass advances v CELLS in one tick.
    v is a distance per tick - exactly what the FG editor's label
    "Movement Speed (cells/{tick})" says - and the quantity that must
    not change is v / tick_hours. That makes it linear in the tick
    length: a flux.

    The loss rule also carries ``if x >= 1.0: return 1.0``, which is
    right for mortality (already lethal in one tick; a longer tick
    cannot be worse, and a shorter one needs r = 1 to still kill
    everything). Applied to a speed it means the FASTEST animals are
    exactly the ones that never rescale. Four of the eight FGs in
    fg_library.yaml sit at 1.0.

    Going 6 h -> 1 h that is a silent six-fold speed-up, produced by
    the one operation whose entire purpose is to hold the biology fixed
    in real time.

104.3 Fix
    RESCALE_RULES["movement_speed"]: "loss" -> "flux". Supersedes 97.5
    on this parameter only; the other four classifications stand.

        pelagic_fish / porpoises / seals / seabirds  1.0 -> 0.16667
        gadoids                                     0.25 -> 0.04167
        zooplankton                                 0.02 -> 0.00333
        phytoplankton / benthic_community            0.0 -> 0.0

    and the 1 h -> 6 h round trip restores every one of them exactly.

    The trade, stated rather than hidden: linear scaling can exceed the
    [0, 1] cap when the tick is made COARSER. A fish crossing a quarter
    cell per hour would cross one and a half in six, which the engine
    cannot express - apply_movement moves biomass to the ADJACENT cell
    or not at all. That is a clamp, it is reported in the confirmation
    dialog (97.5), and it is the honest answer. The loss rule instead
    saturated smoothly towards 1.0, which never reports anything and is
    simply the wrong number.

104.4 Tests
    tests/test_tick_time.py +5:
      * the rule is "flux", 1.0 at 6 h is 1/6 at 1 h, and 0.25 is
        0.04167 rather than the 0.0468 the loss rule gave,
      * cells per hour is invariant over 1..6 h for v = 1.0, 0.25, 0.02,
      * coarsening past the cap clamps AND reports,
      * one more line in test_flux_is_linear.
    Verified by putting "loss" back: 6 cases turn red.

    Headless xvfb GUI run against a SCRATCH library, the path the user
    took: Apply 1 h rescales all eight FGs to exactly v/6 and stamps
    library_metadata.tick_hours = 1; Apply 6 h afterwards restores every
    original value exactly. The real fg_library.yaml was never touched.

    Suite: 698 passed, 12 skipped (was 693/12 after 103).

104.5 Worth a look, not done here
    The same question should be asked of every other entry in
    RESCALE_RULES, since the failure mode was a plausible-looking
    classification rather than a coding error. natural_mortality and
    starve_rate are genuine survival probabilities and compound
    correctly; growth_rate is a fraction ADDED to a stock and
    compounds; max_intake_rate, resting_metabolism and seed_rate are
    per-tick transfers and are linear; seasonal_period is a duration.
    Those all still read correctly against what the engine does with
    them, but none of them has been re-derived from the engine the way
    movement_speed just was.

105 MOVEMENT-SPEED INPUT RANGE FOLLOWS THE TICK LENGTH (2026-09-28)

105.1 Why
    After 104 the values rescale correctly - at a 1 h tick every FG in
    fg_library.yaml sits at or below 1/6 - but the FG editor's input
    still declared [0, 1]. The whole library then lived in the bottom
    sixth of the slider, and the hint label said a speed was allowed
    that is six times the fastest animal the library describes.

105.2 The ceiling is derived, not invented
    _movement_speed_bounds returns

        (0.0, rescale_value("movement_speed", 1.0,
                            DEFAULT_TICK_HOURS, tick_hours)[0])

    i.e. the library's calibrated maximum - one cell per 6 h - put
    through the same flux rule its values take. That is what makes the
    two agree: a library rescaled from 6 h lands exactly ON the ceiling
    rather than being clamped by it, and fg_library.yaml has FOUR FGs at
    the calibration maximum, so "exactly on" is the common case, not a
    corner.

        1 h  [0, 0.167]    3 h  [0, 0.5]
        2 h  [0, 0.333]    6 h  [0, 1]      (unchanged)

    This is NOT the engine's ceiling, and the two are deliberately
    different. state.py clips speed to [0, 1] at any tick length and
    RESCALE_CLAMP keeps 1.0, because apply_movement moves biomass to the
    adjacent cell or not at all. The editor's bound is the tighter,
    biological one; the clamp is the wider, physical one. The comment on
    RESCALE_CLAMP used to say the two were the same and now says where
    they part.

105.3 How a live range was made possible
    A ranged row baked its [lo, hi] into three places at construction:
    the validatecommand closure, the slider's from_/to, and the hint
    label. Re-running the builder would have stacked a second
    trace_add on the same StringVar.

    _build_inline_ranged_entry now owns a MUTABLE ``bounds`` dict that
    both the validator and _bind_slider_entry read, so a range change is
    a mutation plus two widget options - nothing is re-registered. Rows
    opt into this by passing ``key``; they land in self._ranged_rows and
    _set_ranged_row_range changes them. Only "dm:movement_speed" uses it
    today.

    The refresh hangs off _refresh_tick_labels, which every path that
    commits a tick length already calls (Apply, new project, project
    load), so there is no fourth call site to keep in sync.

    _range_label renders the hint: "%g" would have printed the 1 h
    ceiling as 0.166667 and pushed the label out of its fixed-width
    slot, so it is three significant figures, and whole numbers stay
    whole ("[0, 1]", not "[0, 1.00]").

105.4 Tests
    tests/test_tick_time.py +1 (6 cases): the editor's ceiling IS
    rescale_value(1.0, 6, h), it never clamps, and every library value
    rescaled from 6 h stays at or below it. Pure - it does not import
    fgconfig, which needs a display at import time.

    Headless xvfb GUI run against a SCRATCH library, 6 h -> 1 -> 3 -> 2
    -> 6 h, 23 checks at every step:
      * bounds, hint label and BOTH slider end points follow,
      * pelagic_fish lands exactly on the new ceiling,
      * typing half the ceiling into the real Entry is accepted and
        typing 1.5x it is rejected - i.e. the validatecommand reads the
        mutated dict, not a copy taken at construction,
      * writing an out-of-range value to the var does not move the
        slider knob, which is the same check for _bind_slider_entry,
      * and 6 h comes back to exactly [0, 1].
    The real fg_library.yaml was never touched.

    Suite: 704 passed, 12 skipped (was 698/12 after 104).

105.5 Open
    Every other ranged row still declares a constant. max_intake_rate,
    resting_metabolism and seed_rate are fluxes too, so their editor
    ranges have the same mismatch at a short tick - the difference is
    that their library values sit far below the ceiling, so nobody has
    hit it. The mechanism added here is generic; applying it is a
    one-line call per row once someone decides what each ceiling means.

106 TICK LENGTH MOVES FROM THE EDITOR TO THE COMMAND LINE (2026-09-28)

106.1 The decision
    USER DECISION 2026-09-28, reversing the GUI half of section 97:
    fg_library.yaml holds ONE calibration, LIBRARY_TICK_HOURS = 6, and
    the FG editor can no longer change it. A run that wants another tick
    length says so with --tick-length [1, 6] (default 6), and the
    relevant library values are converted IN MEMORY as the run starts.
    Nothing is ever written back.

    This is a better arrangement than what it replaces. Section 97 had
    the tick length in the project file AND a rescaled, stamped library
    shared between projects - two places for one number, with a warning
    dialog to catch them disagreeing. Now the library is a constant and
    the tick length is a run parameter, which cannot disagree with
    anything.

106.2 The conversion was incomplete, and that mattered more now
    ``rescale_params`` only walked a species' TOP-LEVEL keys. Three
    tick-dependent parameters live elsewhere and were never converted:

      handling_time       per (predator, prey). The Holling ceiling is
                          1/h ton prey per ton predator per TICK, so
                          holding the real-time ceiling fixed needs 1/h
                          linear in the tick length, i.e. h inverse in
                          it. New "inverse" rule, x / k.
      max_intake_rate     per-pair override. Flux, like the species-
                          level one. The library has exactly one:
                          porpoises_preys_on_gadoids.
      biomass_factor      per impact_table row. The fraction of standing
                          biomass an impact removes per tick
                          (compute_impact_mortality: contribution =
                          biomass * bf), i.e. a loss rate.

    NOT energy_factor, and the distinction is the point: it enters as
    1 + sum(energy_factor) multiplying the metabolic costs, exactly like
    feeding_cost and movement_cost, which 97.5 already lists as
    tick-independent because they multiply resting_metabolism - which is
    itself rescaled. Scaling the multiplier too would apply the tick
    length twice. Checked in the code rather than assumed; the first
    draft of this section had it in the wrong column.

    a is a flux and h its inverse, so the dimensionless product a*h
    comes out unchanged. That is the algebraic check that the pair is
    classified consistently, and it is asserted.

    As a rare manual action in the editor these gaps mostly did not
    bite. Running on every non-6 h rollout, a missed parameter is a
    silently different biology: the Holling ceiling would have been six
    times too high at --tick-length 1.

106.3 Where the conversion happens
    lib/world/tick_time.py
        LIBRARY_TICK_HOURS = 6, kept separate from DEFAULT_TICK_HOURS
        because they answer different questions ("what is the library
        calibrated at" vs "what does a run use when not told").
        New "inverse" rule; INTERACTION_RESCALE_RULES and
        IMPACT_ROW_RESCALE_RULES; rescale_species(), which walks the
        nested structures and COPIES every level it changes - those
        dicts are the library's own objects, shared with other species
        and with the next call. add_tick_length_argument(), shared so
        the entry points cannot drift on the flag name or the bounds.

    lib/config/config_loader.py
        load_project_config and setup_full_mareld_mvp take tick_hours
        and call rescale_species just before FunctionalGroup. A 6 h run
        short-circuits and is bit-identical to not calling it.
        project_tick_hours and library_tick_hours are GONE.

    The flag reaches the workers through the builders, not a global:
    _EnvBuilder / _ProbeEnvBuilder resolve TICK_HOURS in the parent
    process and bake it into the pickled instance, the way
    APPLY_NATURAL_MORTALITY already worked - a spawn-worker re-imports
    train.py and would otherwise get the module default back.
    EnvironmentBuilder carries it for the GPU path.

106.4 Removed
    fgconfig: the Tick Length entry, its Apply button, the live
    validator, _plan_tick_rescale, _format_rescale_plan,
    apply_tick_length, the library stamp, the "Tick Length Mismatch"
    warning, and reading/writing project_metadata.tick_hours. The
    per-tick labels stay and now always read "6 h"; get_tick_hours
    returns LIBRARY_TICK_HOURS. The movement-speed input range from
    section 105 is a constant [0, 1] again, since the editor no longer
    has a tick length to follow - the mechanism that made the range
    live is kept, unused, because it cost nothing and one row wanting
    it again is a one-line call.

    mareld2.yaml: project_metadata.tick_hours. fg_library.yaml had no
    library_metadata stamp to remove - the editor only wrote one when
    it rescaled, and the shipped library never was.

    Tools: budget_gate and starve_calibration read the library's own
    numbers, which mean LIBRARY_TICK_HOURS by definition, so they use
    the constant and take no flag. viability builds real envs and takes
    the flag.

106.5 Tests
    tests/test_tick_length_runtime.py, 33 cases, written around the
    REAL-TIME quantities rather than the per-tick numbers:
      * the Holling ceiling in ton prey per ton predator per DAY is
        invariant over 1-6 h, on the live library's own pairs (with a
        guard that at least two saturating pairs were checked, so the
        test cannot pass by skipping everything),
      * the dimensionless product a*h is invariant,
      * the per-pair max_intake_rate override is converted,
      * impact mortality over a WEEK is invariant,
      * energy_factor is left alone,
      * the library's dicts are never mutated,
      * a 6 h run returns the input object unchanged,
      * the loader converts the groups and never writes the file,
      * the flag's bounds, default and underscore alias, and that all
        four entry points register it,
      * neither YAML carries a tick length any more.

    Verified by deleting the code under test: removing the
    handling_time rule turns 10 red, removing the loader hook turns 2
    red.

    tests/test_tick_time.py: the loader-accessor cases are gone with the
    accessors; the section 105 ceiling case is reframed as what it now
    is - no library speed is clamped by the engine's [0, 1] cap.

    Suite: 731 passed, 12 skipped (was 704/12 after 105).

106.6 Validation
    train.py --tick-length 1, one generation, ok.
    tools/viability.py --tick-length 1 --ticks 40, ok.
    tools/probes/budget_gate.py unchanged output.
    Headless xvfb GUI run against SCRATCH copies, 7 checks: no tick
    entry, no apply_tick_length, no "Tick Length" label anywhere in the
    widget tree, per-tick labels read "/ 6 h", the movement-speed range
    is [0, 1], Apply Changes writes no library stamp and Save Project
    writes no project_metadata.tick_hours.

106.7 Open
    The conversion is applied per species independently, so several
    impacts summing their biomass_factor contributions before the
    biomass update are each compounded on their own. For the small
    rates in the library the difference is below float32 noise, and it
    is the same approximation natural_mortality already lives with next
    to the other losses - but it is an approximation, not an identity,
    and nobody has measured where it stops being negligible.

107 THE GPU VIEWER NEVER GOT --tick-length (2026-09-28)

107.1 The report and the arithmetic
    Training with a 500-tick rollout at --tick-length 1, the status bar
    read "0 y 125 d" at the end of the probe. 125 d is 3000 h, and
    3000 / 500 = 6: the viewer was converting at the LIBRARY's tick
    length, not the run's.

    Not a display bug in the conversion - the engine really did run at
    1 h, only the clock above it did not. 500 ticks at 1 h is 20 d.

107.2 Cause
    lib/gpu/visual.py builds its own LiveVisualizer and section 106
    never passed it a tick length, so it took the default. train.py,
    inference.py and api.py were wired; the GPU viewer was not.

    The reason it got missed is worth more than the fix. Section 101
    added a test that every entry point passes ``tick_hours``, and that
    test carried a HAND-WRITTEN list of three files - the three I had
    edited. It could not fail for a file that was not on it. A test
    that enumerates its own subjects only ever checks what its author
    already remembered.

107.3 Fix
    lib/gpu/visual.py passes
    ``tick_hours=getattr(builder, "tick_hours", LIBRARY_TICK_HOURS)``.
    From the BUILDER, not by re-reading the flag: the builder is what
    actually applies --tick-length to the library, so the clock and the
    biology cannot disagree even if one of them is wired wrongly later.

    tests/test_viz_sim_time.py now DISCOVERS the construction sites -
    every *.py at the root and under lib/ that constructs a
    LiveVisualizer - and classifies them:

      pass the flag   train.py, inference.py, lib/gpu/visual.py
      no flag exists  api.py, manual_play.py, simple_inference.py

    with a separate test asserting the discovered set equals the
    classified set, so a new call site fails until someone says which
    kind it is. The value check from section 106 is kept: passing
    ``tick_hours`` is not enough, the expression has to mention
    ``tick_length`` (or the builder's ``tick_hours``), because a call
    passing a constant or a stale accessor would satisfy "the keyword
    is present" just as well.

    Verified by deleting the fix: the GPU case turns red on its own.

107.4 Validation
    train_gpu.py --tick-length 1 --visual, one generation on CPU/eager,
    completed. Suite: 734 passed, 12 skipped (was 731/12 after 106).

107.5 Note on which rollout the number describes
    The two trainers take their probe length from different flags -
    train.py from --n_eval_ticks (via the viewer's "Probe ticks"
    slider, which the user can override live) and train_gpu.py's
    progress evaluation from --eval-ticks, default 1000. Worth knowing
    when a displayed duration looks unexpected: check which rollout the
    tick counter is counting before concluding the clock is wrong.

108 NATURAL MORTALITY RE-DERIVED AS RESIDUAL M1 (2026-09-28)

108.1 The question and the answer
    Asked whether the per-FG `natural_mortality` values match the
    literature, given the intended meaning: the fraction of a one-ton
    population that dies of AGE per 6 h tick, multiplied by the current
    biomass at runtime, on top of starvation, predation and impacts.

    They did not, and the reason is structural rather than arithmetic.
    Section 35.3 derived them from an allometric scaling of NATURAL
    mortality, ~M^-0.25. The published M values that scaling is anchored
    to - ICES M = 0.2/yr for cod, 0.3-0.5/yr for herring, quoted in
    section 65 P4 to REJECT a change - are single-species TOTAL natural
    mortality. Total M includes predation. Predation is explicit in the
    tick (`_apply_predation`, Holling II with Beddington-DeAngelis), so
    the library was charging every prey FG for its predators twice.

    The quantity the parameter should hold is the RESIDUAL: what ICES
    multispecies (SMS) calls M1, i.e. M minus the predation the model
    resolves. That is the single finding; everything below follows from
    it.

108.2 What the old values actually said
    Converted at 1460 ticks/yr (the rescale rule is "loss", so these
    compound: M = -1460 * ln(1 - m), section 104.5):

      FG              m/tick    implied M/yr   annual loss   1/M
      zooplankton     0.0025        3.655          97.4 %    0.27 yr
      pelagic_fish    5e-04         0.730          51.8 %    1.37 yr
      gadoids         2e-04         0.292          25.3 %    3.42 yr
      porpoises       5e-05         0.073           7.0 %   13.70 yr
      seals           4e-05         0.058           5.7 %   17.12 yr
      seabirds        2e-04         0.292          25.3 %    3.42 yr

    Read as total M the fish rows are defensible. Read as residual M1 -
    which is what the engine needs - pelagic_fish is 4-7x too harsh and
    gadoids 2-6x.

    The unambiguous error is the last row. seabirds and gadoids carried
    the SAME value, so seabirds got a mean lifespan of 3.4 years. The
    W^-0.25 line does not cross the ectotherm/endotherm boundary:
    birds and mammals sit far below the fish line at equal mass.
    Common guillemots live 20-30 years. This row is wrong under every
    reading of the parameter, M or M1.

108.3 Two definitions were in circulation
    The user's statement is "age only". Section 35.3 says the term
    represents "senescence, hidden predation, disease, mechanical
    damage, sedimentation". Those differ by a factor of several.

    Settled on the M1 reading - age, disease, and predation by
    UNMODELLED predators - because it is the only one with published
    numbers attached. Pure senescence is not separately measurable for
    fish or copepods; nobody reports it. A strict age-only reading would
    push every value lower still, and would have no source to check it
    against. The library comments now state the M1 reading next to each
    value so the next person does not have to re-derive which of the two
    a number was set under.

108.4 Biomass pool, not head count
    `_apply_decision_maker_population_change` applies
    `keep = 1 - m * mortality_multiplier` to `fg.biomass` and
    `fg.energy_reserve`. It is a BIOMASS pool. Numbers-weighted M is
    dominated by juveniles - high mortality, little mass - while
    biomass-weighted M is dominated by adults. The values should
    therefore lean adult, which pushes all of them DOWN relative to a
    population-average M, not up. The seal row is the visible case: the
    new value sits above adult-only grey-seal survival (0.94-0.97) but
    below a pup-inclusive average, because the pool carries subadults.

108.5 New values
      FG              old      new      M1/yr   annual   literature band
      zooplankton     0.0025   0.0025   3.655   97.4 %   (left alone)
      pelagic_fish    5e-04    1.0e-04  0.146   13.6 %   M1 0.1-0.2
      gadoids         2e-04    7.0e-05  0.102    9.7 %   M1 0.05-0.15
      porpoises       5e-05    6.0e-05  0.088    8.4 %   0.07-0.12
      seals           4e-05    6.0e-05  0.088    8.4 %   0.05-0.12
      seabirds        2e-04    8.0e-05  0.117   11.0 %   0.08-0.15

    Bands are ranges, not point citations: the fish rows should be
    checked against the North Sea SMS keyrun M1 tables in the ICES
    WGSAM reports before anyone quotes a single number from them. The
    endotherm rows come from reported adult annual survival - harbour
    porpoise 0.90-0.95 excluding bycatch, grey seal 0.94-0.97, common
    guillemot and razorbill 0.90-0.95, kittiwake 0.85-0.88.

    Two rows went UP. porpoises was already the one FG whose allometric
    value landed inside its band; 5e-05 to 6e-05 moves it to the middle
    of that band rather than correcting it. seals was below its band for
    a biomass pool, per 108.4.

108.6 Zooplankton was left alone on purpose
    0.0025/tick is 0.01/day, M 3.655/yr, a 69-day half-life. That is at
    the LOW end of measured copepod mortality, so it is not the value
    making the group fragile. Section 74.7 already located the real
    squeeze on the other side of the ledger: the anabolic ceiling is
    growth_rate * (satiation_scale - u_x) = 0.01 * 0.3 = 0.003/tick, a
    headroom of 1.2 over this mortality before any predation lands. A
    copepod P/B of 15-40/yr in temperate shelf seas against the 4.4/yr
    this growth_rate can reach says `growth_rate` is the low number, not
    `natural_mortality` the high one. Lowering zoo mortality would hide
    that, so it was not done. CLOSED by section 111: growth_rate is now
    0.0913, set from the literature (copepod P/B 15-40/yr) rather than
    from what the model tolerates. The mortality value here is unchanged
    and still correct; the headroom it leaves is 11.0, in line with
    every other DM.

108.7 Scope and consequences
    * `--mortality` is OFF by default (`APPLY_NATURAL_MORTALITY = False`
      in train.py), so no existing default run changes behaviour. Only
      `--mortality on` runs see any of this.
    * `--mortality_multiplier` (section 88) scales every FG by the same
      factor, so it cannot express this change; per-FG values stay a
      library edit, which is what this was.
    * Checkpoints are NOT invalidated - no observation shape, FG set,
      grid layout or Observability entry moved. But policies trained
      with `--mortality on` under the old values learned a harsher
      world for pelagic_fish, gadoids and seabirds than the one they
      would now be evaluated in.
    * `fg_library.yaml` is calibrated at LIBRARY_TICK_HOURS = 6 as
      always; the loader compounds these under `--tick-length` via the
      "loss" rule (sections 103, 106). Nothing here is tick-specific.
    * The viability verdict is unaffected: VIABILITY.md's normative
      corner runs `--mortality off`, so no recorded survivor fraction
      there was produced under the old values. `tools/viability.py
      --mortality on` and `tools/probes/budget_gate.py` DO apply them,
      and any number quoted from those two before today predates this
      change. Lower mortality can only make the world easier, so the
      direction of a past verdict is safe; the magnitudes are stale.
    * The FG editor rewrites fg_library.yaml WITHOUT comments. If the
      justifications added next to these six values disappear, the file
      went through the editor, and the numbers are no longer evidenced
      by anything but this section. Same failure mode the phytoplankton
      `seed_rate: 0.0` comment guards against (section 74.11).

108.8 Validation
    Suite: 734 passed, 12 skipped (unchanged from 107 - no test asserts
    a library mortality value; tests/test_mortality_multiplier.py and
    tests/test_gpu_ecosystem.py build their own FGs). One-generation
    training run on mareld2.yaml with --mortality on completed.

109 ZOOPLANKTON GROWTH IS STABILITY-LIMITED, NOT LITERATURE-LIMITED (2026-09-28)
-------------------------------------------------------------------------------

109.1 What this closes
    Section 108.6 left `growth_rate: 0.01` open with the observation
    that it caps zooplankton at P/B 4.4/yr against a literature range
    of 15-40/yr for copepods in temperate shelf seas. Asked to raise
    it. It was raised, to 0.0125 - a fifth of the way to the bottom of
    that range, and not one step further, because the world does not
    tolerate more. This section is the measurement that says so.

109.2 What the parameter can reach
    `_apply_decision_maker_population_change` computes

      biomass_delta = B * growth_rate * (s_X - u_X)

    and intake shuts off as s_X approaches `satiation_scale`, so the
    anabolic ceiling is

      growth_rate * (satiation_scale - u_X) = growth_rate * 0.3

    with the zooplankton defaults (u_X = 0.5, satiation_scale absent ->
    HUNGER_SATIATION_SCALE = 0.8). Growth does NOT draw down
    `energy_reserve`; the reserve is only scaled on loss. The cost is
    implicit instead: s_X = E/(B * ME), so added biomass dilutes the
    per-ton reserve, and that dilution is the negative feedback.
    Reaching P/B 15-40/yr therefore needs growth_rate 0.034-0.091.

109.3 The sweep
    SUPERSEDED BY 111 - read that first. The measurement below stands
    and is part of the diagnosis in 111, but its CONCLUSION does not:
    a stability sweep may diagnose a parameter, it may not set one that
    has a literature value. growth_rate is now 0.0913, from copepod
    P/B, and the cliff this section found is the model defect rather
    than a bound on the biology. (110 moved the value to 0.0208 on the
    same mistaken footing.)
    Frozen greedy behaviour, colocated spawn, no ARS - the viability
    rig's machinery (`install_behaviour`, `colocate_spawn`,
    `run_rollout`), 60x60, 3000 ticks, `--mortality off`, which is the
    normative corner in VIABILITY.md and the training default. Columns
    are final-window means over spawn level, except *_min which is the
    trough over the whole horizon. realPB is the REALISED
    production/biomass, growth_rate * <s_X - u_X>_B * 1460, not the
    ceiling.

      g        realPB  <surp>  phy_end phy_min  zoo_end zoo_min  gad_end
      0.01      3.6    0.245     0.53    0.28     1.94    0.45     1.85
      0.0125    4.4    0.240     0.60    0.16     2.13    0.28     1.39
      0.015     5.1    0.233     0.47    0.04     2.27    0.07     0.57
      0.02      6.5    0.225     0.45    0.01     1.85    0.01     0.23
      0.025     7.8    0.213     0.47    0.01     2.22    0.005    0.21
      0.03      8.9    0.202     0.32    0.007    1.95    0.004    0.10

    (0.02/0.025/0.03 and the 0.01 row are 2-seed means, 0.0125 and
    0.015 are 3 seeds;
    per-seed spread is small - at 0.0125 phy_min was 0.132/0.158/0.186.)

    The cliff is between 0.0125 and 0.015. It is not a gradual
    softening: at 0.015 the phytoplankton trough drops by a factor 4
    and gadoids fall from 1.4 to 0.57 of their spawn level. That last
    column is the tell - this is a trophic CASCADE, not a
    predator-prey oscillation. Zooplankton itself survives every arm;
    what breaks is what sits above it.

    0.0125 was chosen as the last value on the safe side. It buys +22 %
    realised P/B (3.6 -> 4.4/yr) and costs phy_min 0.28 -> 0.16. That
    is the whole of what was available.

109.4 The mortality setting moves the cliff, and by a lot
    The same sweep with `--mortality on` puts the cliff between 0.02
    and 0.035 instead - 0.02 held phy_min at 0.21 and gadoids at 1.9,
    and only 0.035 produced the cascade. The 0.0025/tick drain on
    zooplankton damps the oscillation, so mortality-on is the PERMISSIVE
    setting here, which is the opposite of the intuition that mortality
    makes a world harsher.

    The library is one calibration for both settings, so it has to be
    set to the stricter one. 108.6's numbers (ceiling 0.003/tick,
    headroom 1.2, P/B 4.4) are arithmetic and hold under either
    setting; what does NOT transfer is the CLIFF LOCATION, which is a
    rollout result and moves by a factor 2 between them. Anyone quoting
    a cliff has to say which mortality setting measured it.

109.5 The binding constraint is phytoplankton, and it has a defence
    One run settles where the limit lives. At zoo growth_rate 0.035 -
    two steps past the cliff - with phytoplankton `growth_rate` doubled
    from 0.0375 to 0.075 and nothing else changed. NOTE: this pair was
    measured under `--mortality on`, the permissive setting, before the
    sweep moved to `off`, and was not rerun. The direction is not in
    doubt; the magnitudes belong to that setting.

                          phy_min  zoo_min  gad_end
      phyto 0.0375 (now)    0.011    0.005     0.33
      phyto 0.075           0.026    0.228     0.937

    The cascade disappears. zoo_min rises by a factor 40 and gadoids
    return to their spawn level. Zooplankton's growth_rate was never
    the constraint; the phytoplankton supply is.

    SUPERSEDED BY 113: 0.0375 IS wrong, and for the reason 108 found
    in natural_mortality - it is a rate measured net of the grazing the
    tick applies itself. The defence below is what the repo believed at
    the time and is kept for that reason.

    Section 63.3 set it deliberately: net mu ~ 0.15/day, mid-range for
    HELCOM / Behrenfeld-Falkowski temperate net growth, explicitly NOT
    the Eppley physiological maximum of ~0.125/tick, precisely because
    the physiological maximum drives phyto to carrying capacity and
    masks the grazing signal. A phytoplankton P/B of 100-300/yr from
    the literature is a GROSS turnover figure and is not the same
    quantity as this net mu, so the two numbers do not refute each
    other and 0.0375 was not raised here.

    The real gap is that phytoplankton and zooplankton cannot both sit
    at their literature turnover under a plain logistic supply with no
    seasonal forcing. `seasonal_amplitude` is 0.0 in fg_library.yaml
    while 63.3 recommends 0.7 - an unclosed recommendation, and the
    more likely route than pushing net mu past its calibration, since
    a spring bloom supplies the surplus in pulses instead of raising
    the mean. Untested. Next lever, not this change.

109.6 Read-throughs from the sweep that are NOT this change
    * pelagic_fish ends at 0.000-0.002 of spawn in EVERY arm, including
      growth_rate 0.01. It is a pre-existing property of the frozen
      greedy arm (sections 73, 74), not a consequence of anything here.
    * porpoises go extinct in every arm, between tick 430 and 2450, at
      every growth_rate. Same story.
    * The phytoplankton dead-cell count stayed at 1 - the single
      inaccessible cell - in every rollout in this arc, at every
      growth_rate and
      both mortality settings. `seed_rate: 0.0` makes exact zero
      absorbing, so this was the failure mode the sweep was built to
      catch, and it did not appear. Section 73.1 explains why: zoo ->
      phyto is the only type III pair in the library, so per-capita
      grazing vanishes faster than linearly and a cell with P > 0
      cannot be grazed to zero asymptotically. The refuge level is
      still indistinguishable from extinction (73.2), which is what
      phy_min measures.
    * Zooplankton now settles at ~2.1x its `mareld2.yaml` spawn level
      and gadoids at ~1.4x. The spawn levels are further from
      equilibrium than they were. Recalibrating them is a follow-up.

109.7 Scope and consequences
    * Checkpoints are NOT invalidated - no observation shape, FG set,
      grid layout or Observability entry moved. Policies trained at
      0.01 learned a slower zooplankton, and pelagic_fish's policy
      learned a slower prey field.
    * The value is calibrated at LIBRARY_TICK_HOURS = 6 like the rest
      of the library; the loader compounds it under `--tick-length` via
      the "growth" rule (sections 103, 106).
    * 108.6's headroom figure is updated: the anabolic ceiling is now
      0.00375/tick against natural_mortality 0.0025/tick, a headroom of
      1.5 where 74.7 measured 1.2. Still thin.
    * The sweep script is not in the repo. It is 90 lines over
      `lib/diagnostics/viability.py`'s public helpers plus a
      `growth_rate` assignment and an `on_tick` hook counting dead
      phytoplankton cells; the table above is its output.

109.8 The viability corner, before and after - read this before
      quoting 109.3
    `tools/viability.py --spawn colocated --behaviour greedy` runs its
    own settings: 20x20, 7300 ticks, 3 seeds, mortality off. That is a
    much smaller grid than the project's 60x60 over more than twice the
    horizon, and it is harsher. Both values were run through it.

      fg              0.01 (before)          0.0125 (after)
      gadoids         FAIL extinct@4717      FAIL extinct@4182
      pelagic_fish    FAIL below floor       FAIL below floor
      porpoises       FAIL extinct@1386      FAIL extinct@926
      benthic         PASS  eq/spawn 0.260   PASS  eq/spawn 0.354
      phytoplankton   PASS  min 0.196        PASS  min 0.093
      zooplankton     PASS  min 0.339        PASS  min 0.175
      overall         NOT VIABLE             NOT VIABLE

    The verdict does not change and the three failing groups are the
    same three, failing in the same way - this corner has been NOT
    VIABLE since well before this change (sections 73, 74, 90-92). But
    the change is not free there either: the phytoplankton trough
    halves, and the two groups that were already going extinct go
    extinct 10-30 % earlier.

    So the margin claim in 109.3 is grid-dependent and must be quoted
    with its grid. On 60x60 - the project's reference grid, and the one
    mareld2.yaml is drawn for - 0.0125 is comfortably on the safe side
    of the cliff. On the rig's 20x20 default over 7300 ticks it is not
    comfortable, it is merely not the thing that broke the corner.
    Anyone who wants the old margin back should set growth_rate to
    0.01; the change buys +22 % realised P/B and nothing else.

    SUPERSEDED BY 111: growth_rate is now 0.0913, set from copepod P/B
    and not from any margin. The trade-off weighed here - margin against
    turnover - is not a trade-off that should have been made at a
    biological parameter.

109.9 Validation
    Suite: 734 passed, 12 skipped (unchanged). One-generation training
    run on mareld2.yaml completed. Viability corner run at both values,
    table above.

110 ZOOPLANKTON GROWTH CALIBRATED IN THE MORTALITY-ON WORLD (2026-09-28)
------------------------------------------------------------------------

110.1 The instruction, and what it does NOT mean
    Section 109 chose `growth_rate: 0.0125` by sweeping a world with
    `--mortality off`, on the ground that off is the normative corner in
    VIABILITY.md and train.py's default. The instruction here is that
    growth_rate has to take natural_mortality into account - i.e. the
    parameter cannot be judged in a world where the drain it has to
    outpace is switched off. That is correct, and it is why the sweep
    was redone with mortality on.

    It does NOT mean that natural_mortality belongs inside growth_rate.
    It does not, and nothing in the engine changed. The tick applies

      B' = B * (1 - natural_mortality) * (1 + growth_rate * (s_X - u_X))

    - mortality first, to `biomass` AND `energy_reserve` so that
    s_X = E/(B*ME) is untouched by it, then growth on the reduced
    biomass. `growth_rate` is a GROSS rate, growth before death, and
    always was. Senescence is a separate subtraction. An earlier draft
    of this section presented the value as "solved net of
    natural_mortality", which reads as if the drain were being added
    into the parameter and then removed again by the engine. It is not,
    and that framing has been removed: under the gross model
    growth_rate is not a function of natural_mortality at all.

    What IS a function of natural_mortality is where the stability
    cliff sits, and that is the whole of this section.

110.2 The invariant is the NET ceiling, and the cliff does not move
    Section 109.4 recorded that the mortality setting moves the cliff by
    a factor 2 - between 0.0125 and 0.015 with mortality off, between
    0.02 and 0.035 with it on - and treated that as a reason to
    calibrate against the stricter setting. It is not. The cliff does
    not move at all; it was being measured in the wrong coordinate.

    What the tick actually applies per tick is

      net = growth_rate * (satiation_scale - u_X) - natural_mortality

    since `_apply_decision_maker_population_change` scales biomass by
    `1 - natural_mortality` and then adds `B * growth_rate * (s_X -
    u_X)`. Re-reading both sweeps in that coordinate (60x60, 3000 ticks,
    frozen greedy, colocated; phy_min is the phytoplankton trough over
    the horizon, gad the gadoid final window over spawn):

      mortality   g        net/tick   phy_min   gad_end   verdict
      off         0.01     0.00300      0.28      1.85    safe
      off         0.0125   0.00375      0.16      1.39    safe
      off         0.015    0.00450      0.04      0.57    cascade
      off         0.02     0.00600      0.01      0.23    cascade
      on          0.02     0.00350      0.21      1.92    safe
      on          0.0208   0.00374      0.18      1.72    safe
      on          0.025    0.00500      0.02      0.66    cascade
      on          0.03     0.00650      0.01      0.39    cascade

    Sorted by the middle column the two settings interleave exactly.
    Every arm at net <= 0.00375 is safe and every arm at net >= 0.0045
    cascades, with no exception and no dependence on which setting
    produced the number. The cliff is a property of the NET anabolic
    ceiling, at 0.004-0.005/tick, and the gross growth_rate is not the
    quantity that governs anything.

    This is also the check on the reading: 0.0125-with-mortality-off and
    0.0208-with-mortality-on are not merely both "safe", they land in
    the same regime on every column (phy_min 0.16 vs 0.18, zoo_min 0.28
    vs 0.35, gadoids 1.39 vs 1.72). Two different worlds agreeing that
    closely is what a correct invariant looks like.

110.3 The value, and why it is not double counting
    SUPERSEDED BY 111: 0.0208 was still the model's tolerance rather
    than the literature's number. The "not double counting" argument
    below is unaffected and still correct - growth_rate is gross,
    mortality is a separate subtraction - and 111.2 restates it.
    0.0208 is a MEASURED stability limit, read off the mortality-on rows
    of 110.2 exactly the way 109 read 0.0125 off the mortality-off rows:
    0.0208 safe, 0.025 cascade. It is not derived from
    natural_mortality. The net-ceiling identity

      0.0208 * 0.3 - 0.0025 = 0.00374 ~= 0.0125 * 0.3 - 0

    is the EXPLANATION for why the on-world tolerates more gross growth
    than the off-world, not the source of the number. Read the other way
    round it is also the check that 110.2's invariant holds.

    Gross and net side by side, because the two are easy to conflate:

      ceiling     9.1/yr gross    5.5/yr net of mortality
      delivered   7.4/yr gross    3.7/yr net of mortality

    The gross figures are the ones with a biological reference. Copepod
    P/B of 15-40/yr is a GROSS production figure - production is the
    quantity mortality balances in steady state, not a quantity net of
    it - so the delivered 7.4/yr is still 2-5x BELOW its reference.
    Double counting would put the value above that reference; it is a
    factor of several under it. It stays out of reach for the reason
    109.5 gives, the phytoplankton supply, and nothing here revisits
    that.

    The reason 0.01 is not the better value is the same arithmetic run
    forward instead of backward. With mortality on it delivers a net of

      (1 - 0.0025) * (1 + 0.01 * 0.3) - 1 = 0.00049/tick = 0.72/yr

    A copepod population whose gross production barely outpaces its own
    senescence cannot fund any predation at all, which is a biological
    statement and not a tuning one - and 110.4 is what it looks like in
    a rollout. No other FG's growth_rate was touched; 108's mortality
    pass left every growth_rate alone and so does this one.

110.4 The viability corner says the old value was the broken one
    `tools/viability.py --spawn colocated --behaviour greedy
    --mortality on` (20x20, 7300 ticks, 3 seeds), which is now the
    setting the library is calibrated for:

      fg              0.01 (before)              0.0208 (after)
      gadoids         FAIL extinct@4003          FAIL extinct@4461
      pelagic_fish    FAIL below floor           FAIL below floor
      porpoises       FAIL extinct@416           FAIL extinct@918
      benthic         PASS eq 0.356              PASS eq 0.266
      phytoplankton   PASS min 0.139             PASS min 0.067
      zooplankton     FAIL still drifting        PASS eq 1.877
                           eq 0.413, drift 10.5       drift 1.02
      overall         NOT VIABLE                 NOT VIABLE

    One failing group fewer, and the failure that was removed is
    zooplankton's own: at 0.01 with mortality on it never settles -
    drift 10.5 over the last window - and sits at 0.41 of spawn on its
    way down. At 0.0208 it is stationary at 1.88 of spawn. The two
    groups that go extinct in both arms do so 10-120 % LATER.

    The cost is the phytoplankton trough, 0.139 -> 0.067. The overall
    verdict is unchanged and still driven by the same three pre-existing
    failures (sections 73, 74, 90-92).

110.5 The library now presupposes `--mortality on`, and two things
      in the repo say otherwise
    RESOLVED BY 111.5 - do not act on this section. At growth_rate
    0.0913 the value is indistinguishable under the two mortality
    settings, so it presupposes neither and the `--mortality` default
    is a free design decision again. The reasoning below was correct
    for 0.0208 and is kept for that reason only.
    A growth_rate solved net of a drain is WRONG when the drain is off.
    At 0.0208 with `--mortality off` the net ceiling is 0.00624/tick,
    which by the table in 110.2 is two steps past the cliff: phytoplankton
    trough 0.01 of spawn, gadoids at 0.23.

    Two things in the repo contradict the calibration:

      train.py:39   APPLY_NATURAL_MORTALITY = False
      VIABILITY.md  the normative corner runs --mortality off

    Neither was changed here: flipping a default changes every run that
    does not pass the flag, and which way Mareld is modelled - closed
    box or not - is a design decision on record as a deliberate open
    question (35.3, and VIABILITY.md section 1). But it is a blocker
    rather than ambient worry: running `python3 train.py --project
    mareld2.yaml` with no flag now trains against a zooplankton whose
    growth is compensating for a mortality that is not being applied.
    Either the default flips or the library goes back to 0.0125.

110.6 The other decision makers are not squeezed
    Headroom = ceiling / natural_mortality, under the section 108
    mortality values:

      FG              growth   sat-u   ceiling    nm       headroom
      zooplankton     0.0208    0.30   0.00624   0.0025      2.5
      pelagic_fish    0.004     0.30   0.00120   1.0e-04    12.0
      gadoids         0.0025    0.30   0.00075   7.0e-05    10.7
      porpoises       0.001     0.87   0.00087   6.0e-05    14.5
      seals           0.001     0.30   0.00030   6.0e-05     5.0
      seabirds        0.001     0.30   0.00030   8.0e-05     3.8

    The zooplankton row is superseded by 111.5: growth_rate is 0.0913
    and the headroom is 11.0, which makes it unremarkable rather than
    the outlier. Every other row still holds.

    Zooplankton was the squeezed case and still is, at 2.5 against 1.2
    before section 109 - the mortality it carries is 25-40x any other
    group's, which is what the short copepod lifespan means. Nothing
    else needs the same treatment; seabirds at 3.8 is the next lowest
    and is not near the 1.x range where 74.7 found the problem.

110.7 Scope and consequences
    * Checkpoints are NOT invalidated - no observation shape, FG set,
      grid layout or Observability entry moved. Policies trained at 0.01
      or 0.0125 learned a slower zooplankton.
    * Calibrated at LIBRARY_TICK_HOURS = 6; the loader compounds it
      under `--tick-length` via the "growth" rule (103, 106).
    * 109 is NOT superseded. Its mortality-off cliff measurement stands
      and is half the evidence for 110.2; what 109 got wrong was
      treating the gross parameter as the thing to calibrate.
    * The zooplankton headroom figure in 108.6 is now 2.5.

110.8 Validation
    Suite: 734 passed, 12 skipped (unchanged). One-generation training
    run on mareld2.yaml with `--mortality on` completed. Viability
    corner run at both values with `--mortality on`, table in 110.4.

111 ZOOPLANKTON GROWTH SET FROM THE LITERATURE (2026-09-28)
------------------------------------------------------------

111.1 The principle, and what 109 and 110 got wrong
    Both previous sections chose `growth_rate` by asking what the
    simulation tolerates: 109 swept it and stopped at the stability
    cliff (0.0125, mortality off), 110 redid the sweep in the
    mortality-on world and stopped at that cliff instead (0.0208). Both
    numbers are correct answers to "what does the model tolerate".

    That was the wrong question. AGENTS.md section 7 states the rule -
    biology drives, fix parameters and mechanisms rather than tuning
    around them - and it applies to the biological parameters
    themselves, not only to reward shaping. The zooplankton growth rate
    is what the literature says it is. If the simulation collapses at
    that value, the collapse is a defect in the model and the job is to
    locate it, not to lower the biology until the symptom goes away.

    Restated as a rule for the next person: a stability sweep may
    DIAGNOSE a parameter, it may not SET one that has a literature
    value. 109 and 110 are kept because their measurements are the
    diagnosis; their conclusions are superseded.

111.2 The value
    `growth_rate` is the anabolic ceiling: growth_rate *
    (satiation_scale - u_X) is the most biomass the group can add in a
    tick, reached only at full energy surplus. Copepod P/B in temperate
    shelf seas is 15-40/yr.

    The ceiling is set at the TOP of that band, not its centre, because
    a ceiling at the centre would make the upper half of the literature
    range unreachable by construction - the same category error as
    reading a realised field statistic onto a physiological maximum.
    The library already separates the two that way: porpoise
    `max_intake_rate` is a physiological ceiling and the realised ration
    is the check on it, not the same number (section 92).

      growth_rate = 40 / (0.3 * 1460) = 0.0913

    SOURCE, stated as plainly as 108.5 states its mortality bands:
    "copepod P/B 15-40/yr in temperate shelf seas" is a range, not a
    point citation, and it is not sourced in this repo. Before anyone
    quotes it as settled it should be checked against HELCOM / ICES
    zooplankton production assessments for the Baltic and Kattegat, and
    against Hirst & Bunker (2003) for the underlying copepod growth
    rates. If the band moves, this value moves with it - which is the
    point of setting it from the literature rather than from a sweep.

    Gross, not net. The tick applies B * (1 - natural_mortality) *
    (1 + growth_rate * (s_X - u_X)) - mortality first, to `biomass` and
    `energy_reserve` both so that s_X is untouched, then growth on the
    reduced biomass. P/B is likewise a GROSS production figure;
    production is the quantity mortality balances in steady state, not
    something net of it. The two are the same kind of quantity and
    nothing is subtracted twice.

111.3 What the model does with it
    60x60, 3000 ticks, frozen greedy, colocated spawn, 2 seeds,
    `--mortality on`. ceilPB is growth_rate * 0.3 * 1460, realPB is
    growth_rate * <s_X - u_X>_B * 1460, i.e. what is actually delivered:

      g        ceilPB  realPB  phy_min  zoo_min  gad_end
      0.034     14.9    10.7    0.009    0.005     0.40
      0.046     20.1    12.7    0.005    0.004     0.27
      0.057     25.0    13.7    0.003    0.002     0.24
      0.069     30.2    15.1    0.002    0.001     0.19
      0.0913    39.9    17.3    0.001    0.001     0.18

    Two things to read off it.

    First, DELIVERED production saturates at ~17/yr no matter how high
    the ceiling goes. Raising the ceiling from 15 to 40/yr - a factor
    2.7 - buys 10.7 -> 17.3, a factor 1.6, and the mean energy surplus
    falls from 0.21 to 0.13 as the group grazes down its own food. The
    delivered value lands INSIDE the literature band, at its floor. The
    ceiling is not what limits production here; the supply is.

    Second, the ecosystem cost is already paid at the bottom of the
    band. At 0.034 - the literature MINIMUM - the phytoplankton trough
    is 0.009 of spawn and gadoids are at 0.40. There is no value in the
    literature range at which this world is intact. That is the finding:
    the model cannot carry literature zooplankton biology anywhere in
    its published range, so the defect is not a matter of degree.

    The viability corner (`--spawn colocated --behaviour greedy
    --mortality on`, 20x20, 7300 ticks, 3 seeds) records the same thing
    with a verdict attached:

      gadoids          FAIL below floor
      pelagic_fish     FAIL below floor
      porpoises        FAIL extinct@212
      benthic          PASS eq 0.713
      phytoplankton    FAIL below floor   eq 0.066, min 0.000
      zooplankton      PASS eq 0.651
      overall          NOT VIABLE

    Phytoplankton now fails, which it did not at 0.01 or 0.0208 with
    mortality on, nor at 0.0125 with it off (109.8). That is the
    deficiency naming itself.

111.4 Where the defect is
    Not in this parameter, and not in zooplankton at all. Section 109.5
    already ran the experiment: at zoo growth_rate 0.035 with
    phytoplankton `growth_rate` doubled from 0.0375 to 0.075, the
    cascade disappears - zoo_min rises by a factor 40 and gadoids return
    to their spawn level. The supply side is what breaks.

    SUPERSEDED BY 113 - 0.0375 is net of the model's own grazing, so
    "not an oversight" is wrong. The paragraph is kept as the state of
    belief when 111 was written.

    Phytoplankton's 0.0375 is not an oversight either; section 63.3 set
    it to HELCOM net mu ~0.15/day, explicitly NOT the Eppley
    physiological maximum, on the ground that the maximum drives phyto
    to carrying capacity and masks the grazing signal. So the defect is
    structural rather than a wrong number: a plain logistic supply at
    net mu, with `seasonal_amplitude` at 0.0 against 63.3's recommended
    0.7, cannot feed a zooplankton population running at literature P/B.
    A spring bloom delivers the surplus in pulses, which is how a real
    copepod population funds a P/B of 20-40 without grazing its food to
    a refuge level year-round.

    Untested, and deliberately not done here - the instruction was
    zooplankton. It is the next lever, and it is a phytoplankton change,
    not a zooplankton one.

111.5 Two things this closes
    * The mortality-setting blocker from 110.5 is gone. At 0.0913 the
      same rollout under `--mortality off` gives realPB 15.9-16.2 and
      phy_min 0.000-0.001, i.e. indistinguishable from mortality on
      (17.2-17.4, 0.001). natural_mortality of 3.655/yr is simply small
      against a 40/yr ceiling, so the value no longer presupposes
      either setting and the `--mortality` default is once again a free
      design decision rather than something this calibration forces.
    * The headroom squeeze from 74.7 and 108.6 is resolved, and by
      biology rather than by tuning. ceiling / natural_mortality for
      zooplankton is now 11.0, against 12.0 for pelagic_fish, 10.7 for
      gadoids and 14.5 for porpoises. Zooplankton was the outlier at
      1.2; it is now unremarkable. No other FG's growth_rate was
      touched by 108, 109, 110 or this section - the only value that
      has moved in this whole arc is zooplankton's.

111.6 Scope and consequences
    * Checkpoints are NOT invalidated - no observation shape, FG set,
      grid layout or Observability entry moved. Every policy in
      `results/` was trained against a zooplankton 7-9x slower than
      this one, so they are stale in substance if not in shape.
    * `mareld2.yaml`'s zooplankton spawn level is now further from
      equilibrium in the other direction (0.65 of spawn at the corner,
      against 1.88 at 0.0208). Recalibrating spawn is a follow-up.
    * Calibrated at LIBRARY_TICK_HOURS = 6; the loader compounds it
      under `--tick-length` via the "growth" rule (103, 106).
    * The sweep that produced 109.3, 110.2 and 111.3 is now in the repo
      at `tools/probes/zoo_growth_sweep.py` rather than being quoted
      from a scratch directory.

111.7 Validation
    Suite: 734 passed, 12 skipped. One-generation training run on
    mareld2.yaml with `--mortality on` completed. Viability corner run
    at the new value, table in 111.3.

112 IS THE DEFICIENCY A MISSING BIRTH TERM? NO - THREE NEGATIVE RESULTS
------------------------------------------------------------------------

112.1 The question
    Section 111 left the model unable to carry literature zooplankton
    biology and pointed at the phytoplankton supply without naming a
    mechanism. The question put to it: functional groups reproduce in
    reality - is mortality modelled but birth not?

    Short answer: birth IS modelled, the asymmetries a reader notices
    are real, and none of them is the deficiency. Recorded here so the
    next person does not spend an arc on it.

112.2 Birth is the growth term
    For a decision maker, `_apply_decision_maker_population_change`
    computes

      biomass_delta = B * growth_rate * (s_X - u_X)

    That IS reproduction: in a biomass model recruitment and somatic
    growth are the same quantity, and gating it on the energy surplus
    is what makes it conditional on food the way spawning is. Section
    111 sets its ceiling from copepod P/B, which is a production figure
    - births included. There is no separate birth term missing.

112.3 But three NDM/DM asymmetries are real
                              NDM (phyto, benthos)   DM (all others)
      production              logistic toward K      g * (s_X - u_X)
      seasonality             YES                    NO
      recolonisation          YES (`seed_rate`)      NO
      natural mortality       NO (in the logistic)   YES

    `_seasonal_population_rate` is called only from the NDM branch, so
    zooplankton, fish, mammals and birds reproduce at a flat rate all
    year. `seed_rate` is read only in the NDM branch, so a DM cell
    emptied of biomass can only be repopulated by movement - and
    zooplankton's `movement_speed` is 0.02. Both gaps are genuine.

112.4 All three were tested. None of them moves the outcome.
    Zooplankton at the section 111 value 0.0913, 60x60, 3000 ticks,
    frozen greedy, colocated, `--mortality on`, 2 seeds. Ranges are
    over seeds. realPB is delivered production/biomass per year.

      arm                          realPB  phy_end  zoo_end  gad_end
      baseline (111)                 17.3   0.09-0.14  0.75-0.98  0.07-0.30
      phyto seed_rate 1e-07          17.0-17.5  0.06-0.08  0.64-0.65  0.08-0.18
      phyto seasonal_amp 0.7         19.8-19.9  0.02-0.02  0.15-0.34  0.10-0.26
      zoo DM seasonality amp 0.7     17.7-20.0  0.03-0.08  0.40-0.43  0.15-0.23
      zoo DM seasonality amp 0.9     22.4-23.2  0.03-0.52  0.50-2.97  0.18-0.26

      for reference, the supply-side RATE change (109.5):
      phyto growth_rate 0.075        (zoo 0.035)  gadoids 0.937, zoo_min 0.228

    * `seed_rate` on phytoplankton does exactly what it is for - the
      dead-cell count goes from 1 to 0, so recolonisation works - and
      changes nothing else. The crash is not an absorbing-zero problem
      at this grid size; section 73.1's type III grazing already
      protects a cell with P > 0, which 109.6 recorded.
    * Reproductive seasonality, on either side, RAISES delivered
      zooplankton production (17.3 -> 20-23, i.e. toward the middle of
      the literature band that 111 could only reach the floor of) and
      still leaves gadoids at 0.1-0.26. It buys the thing 111 said was
      short and does not buy the food web, because it raises grazing
      pressure in the same breath.
    * Phytoplankton seasonality at amplitude 0.7 makes the MEAN supply
      worse, not better: phy_end 0.09-0.14 -> 0.015-0.024. Winter mu is
      0.3x and the logistic cannot rebound from a grazed trough at that
      rate, so the recommendation in 63.3 is not a fix for this and
      should not be adopted on the strength of it.
    * The DM-seasonality arm put the grazer's peak 365 ticks (a quarter
      year) after phytoplankton's, which is the diapause geometry - the
      spring bloom escaping grazers that have not built up yet. Even
      that timing does not save it.

112.5 What this leaves
    The one intervention that does work is the supply RATE: doubling
    phytoplankton `growth_rate` returns gadoids to their spawn level
    (109.5). So the deficiency is a magnitude problem in mean primary
    production, not a missing mechanism and not a timing problem.
    Adding birth machinery that is genuinely absent does not fix it.

    That sharpens 111.4 rather than replacing it, and it narrows the
    next lever to one thing: phytoplankton's mean production rate,
    0.0375, set in 63.3 to HELCOM net mu and deliberately below the
    Eppley maximum. Either that calibration is too conservative for a
    grazed system - net mu measured in the field is already net OF
    grazing, which would make using it as the input rate a double
    count, and that is worth checking before anything else - or the
    logistic supply itself is the wrong model for primary production
    here.

    ANSWERED BY 113: it is the double count. The rate is net of the
    grazing the tick applies, the 3.33x reduction 63.3 made is the
    grazing fraction, and the behaviour that justified it was the
    Holling bug section 64 fixed. Raising it to 0.125 returns gadoids
    to 0.63-0.95 of spawn at the section 111 zooplankton value.

112.6 Nothing was changed
    This section is diagnosis only. `fg_library.yaml` is untouched by
    it: phytoplankton keeps `seed_rate: 0.0` and `seasonal_amplitude:
    0.0`, no DM gained a seasonal term, and no code in `lib/` was
    modified. The DM-seasonality arm was run by monkey-patching
    `_apply_decision_maker_population_change` inside the probe.

    Probes: `tools/probes/zoo_growth_sweep.py` (gained `--phyto-season`
    and `--phyto-seed`) and `tools/probes/dm_seasonal_reproduction.py`.

113 PHYTOPLANKTON growth_rate IS NET OF THE GRAZING THE MODEL APPLIES (2026-09-29)
-----------------------------------------------------------------------------------

113.1 The finding
    `phytoplankton.growth_rate = 0.0375` is a rate measured NET of
    grazing, used as the input rate of a logistic whose grazing is then
    applied separately in `_apply_predation`. It is the same class of
    error section 108 found in `natural_mortality`, in the same
    direction, on the other side of the energy flow: a literature figure
    that already contains a loss the model resolves explicitly.

    Four independent lines point at it. They are laid out below; the
    library is NOT changed by this section.

113.2 The stated calibration says "net"
    Section 63.3: `growth_rate = 0.0375 (net mu ~ 0.15/day, HELCOM
    avg)`, justified as "0.0375 lands in the middle of HELCOM /
    Behrenfeld-Falkowski NET growth for temperate zones", against
    "growth_rate 0.125/tick (~0.5/day) is Eppley physiological max, not
    sustained net".

    For phytoplankton, "net growth" in a field context is the observed
    population change: division rate minus grazing, sinking and lysis.
    That is the quantity a model computes as an OUTPUT. The logistic's
    r is an input, and it must be gross of every loss the model applies
    itself.

    The one escape route is Behrenfeld-Falkowski, whose VGPM yields NET
    PRIMARY PRODUCTION - net of autotrophic respiration but NOT of
    grazing. Had 63.3 meant an NPP-derived rate there would be no double
    count. It did not: 0.15/day is a turnover of 55/yr, and NPP-based
    phytoplankton turnover in temperate shelf seas is 100-300/yr. The
    value is below any NPP band, so whichever citation was intended the
    number behaves as net-of-grazing. (That 100-300/yr band is itself
    uncited in this repo and should be checked alongside everything
    else here.)

113.3 The size of the reduction is the grazing fraction
    The value before 63.3 was 0.125/tick - section 63.5 records the
    legacy checkpoint as trained under "growth_rate=0.125, h=0, no
    season". So 63.3 divided the rate by 3.33, i.e. removed 70 % of
    production.

    Calbet & Landry (2004) put microzooplankton consumption at 67 % of
    daily phytoplankton growth overall, 60 % in coastal and estuarine
    systems. Calbet (2001) puts mesozooplankton at a mean 22.6 % of PP
    per day, highest in productive coastal systems. A 70 % reduction is
    not a coincidence of calibration; it is the grazing term, subtracted
    once in the parameter and then again in the tick.

113.4 The rate should be residual, not gross - and 0.0375 is neither
    The rule section 108 established: a rate must be gross of the losses
    the model RESOLVES and net of the ones it does not. Applied here,
    the model's `zooplankton` FG is mesozooplankton - copepod P/B,
    section 111 - and microzooplankton is not represented anywhere. So
    the logistic r should be the division rate minus microzooplankton
    grazing, sinking and lysis, but BEFORE the mesozooplankton grazing
    the tick applies.

      Eppley (1972)  mu_max = ln2 * 0.851 * 1.066^T per day
        T =  5 C   0.81/day = 0.203/tick
        T = 10 C   1.12/day = 0.279/tick
        T = 15 C   1.54/day = 0.385/tick

      minus ~60 % unmodelled micro-grazing (Calbet & Landry, coastal):
        T =  5 C   0.32/day = 0.081/tick
        T = 10 C   0.45/day = 0.112/tick
        T = 15 C   0.62/day = 0.154/tick

    The defensible band for this parameter is therefore roughly
    0.081-0.154/tick for Kattegat/Skagerrak surface temperatures, and
    0.125 sits mid-band, at about 11.5 C. Note that 63.3's own
    "Eppley physiological max, 0.5/day" was already a factor ~2 BELOW
    Eppley at 10 C, so the value it was rejecting as a maximum was not
    one.

113.5 The outcome that justified the reduction was a bug
    63.3's operative argument is not a citation but a behaviour:
    0.125 "drives phyto to CC and masks grazing signal". Section 64,
    one arc later, found why. `_apply_predation` had lost the B_prey
    factor from the Holling numerator, so `a_eff` FELL toward zero as
    prey became abundant instead of saturating. For zooplankton eating
    phytoplankton, at carrying capacity B = 24 t/cell with a = 0.25 and
    h = 4:

      buggy    a_eff = a / (1 + a*h*B)     = 0.25 / 25   = 0.010
      correct  a_eff = a*B / (1 + a*h*B)   = 6.0  / 25   = 0.240

    - a factor 24 of missing grazing exactly where 63.3 observed
    "phyto sits at CC". 64 is explicit that zoo -> phyto "escaped the
    symptom in visible training because a*h = 0.25*4 = 1 gave milder
    scaling in practice, but the same bug was present there too".

    So the phytoplankton growth rate was lowered to compensate for
    under-grazing caused by a bug; 64 fixed the bug; the compensation
    was never reverted. That is inference from two sections and the
    arithmetic above, not something 64 states - but it is the only
    reading under which 63.3's behavioural argument makes sense.

113.6 Measured effect
    Zooplankton at its section 111 literature value 0.0913, 60x60,
    3000 ticks, frozen greedy, colocated, `--mortality on`, 2 seeds
    (3 at 0.125). Only `phytoplankton.growth_rate` varies.

      phyto g   mu/day   phy_end   zoo_end   gad_end        zoo realPB
      0.0375     0.15    0.09-0.14  0.75-0.98  0.07-0.30      17.3
      0.075      0.30    0.15-0.16  2.58-2.60  0.47-0.74      17.8-18.1
      0.1125     0.45    0.31-0.32  4.51       0.35-0.70      17.4-17.6
      0.125      0.50    0.32-0.33  5.06-5.11  0.63-0.95      17.4-17.5
      0.15       0.60    0.35-0.41  5.87-6.03  0.74-0.99      18.9-20.4

    Gadoids go from 0.07-0.30 of spawn to 0.63-0.95 and phytoplankton's
    own final level triples. The cascade section 111 could not avoid at
    any literature zooplankton value is gone at a phytoplankton value
    that is itself from the literature.

    Read the last column carefully: zooplankton's DELIVERED production
